Both modes now clear the Lc 45 non-text floor with room (74.7 light, 66.3 dark). **And since 2026-08-26 the pick is GUARDED (the bright-brand work; LOG):** a bright brand's picked step cannot clear the floor at any vividness — yellow's solid on white is the canonical case — so when the pick misses any of the ring's three beds the emission solves down the hue instead, to the most chromatic placement of the brand's hue that clears every bed (the glyph solve with the ring's own beds; Apple's own arrangement — bright fill, darker cut of the same hue wherever the brand must be a line). A brand whose pick clears ships the `var()` unchanged, byte for byte, and a law pins the branch logic in both directions.

**"Defined once" always meant one designed VALUE, not one selector — and the heading was already wrong before TextField.** The ring lands wherever focus can: the control, an interactive surface (`.kui-surface:where(button, a)`, since card-as-button), and the field wrapper. That last is the system's one deliberate departure from `:focus-visible`: **a field's focus is a mode, not a keyboard affordance.** You do not press a field, you enter it, and once your keystrokes land inside, the box has to say so however you arrived — which is what every native text input does. The ring belongs to the box that draws the border, so it encloses the slots.

**It rings on the INPUT holding focus, not on `:focus-within` (narrowed 2026-08-05).** `:focus-within` fires for any descendant, and once a control hosted in a slot became a first-class pattern (section 4) that descendant is routinely a button — so tabbing to a clear button lit the field's ring *and* the button's own, one nested inside the other, saying two different things. Worse, the field claimed a focus it did not have, which empties the ring of the only meaning it has: *your keystrokes land here*. The question the selector asks moved from "is focus anywhere in this box" to "is the caret in this field"; a hosted control rings itself, as any control does. The law is therefore not a count of rules — counting one file is exactly what let this claim go stale — but that **every focus rule in every shipped stylesheet resolves the same three tokens**, asserted across all of them and mutation-checked.

### Invalid: a role remap, and only the border moves (decided 2026-08-04)

Same mechanism as disabled below, for the same reason: the control keeps its shape and changes family, so the pairing stays designed and testable instead of a red border bolted onto whatever was underneath. It lives in the **shared control layer**, not in the component that first needed it — Select, Combobox and NumberField can all be wrong — and it is a STATE, never a variant: nothing chooses to look invalid, which is why there is no `invalid` prop. Two spellings are read, both required: `data-invalid`, which Base UI writes inside a `Field.Root`, and `aria-invalid`, the platform spelling standalone. A `:has()` arm covers the controls whose visible box is a wrapper around the input, where the state lands on a child and the border being corrected is the parent's.

**The value the user typed is their content and stays legible; the BOX carries the state** — border and ring both, through one `--invalid-edge` token picked per mode (`--destructive-solid` light, `--destructive-11` dark). Disabled outranks invalid by specificity, deterministically, and a mounted law pins it.

**A CHECKED mark's fill joins the state (2026-08-08, Kushagra — judged in the playground): the wash, not the shout.** An accent tick inside a destructive border was two chromatic signals arguing on one control — this section's own 2026-08-04 argument, unfinished by one property. The ecosystem splits three ways (Material 3 and Spectrum paint the checked error box in the full error solid — verified in Flutter's M3 defaults and spectrum-css; Chakra keeps the accent fill inside a red border; Apple/Radix/Ant never paint the control), and the full solid was rejected as a shout: the error takes the control **the way disabled does** — destructive's SOFT trio on the fill, the family's designed label-on-soft pairing on the glyph, the solid's light catch stood down (a gradient over a wash is fog, §19). Checked only: an unchecked invalid mark keeps its seal and wears the edge (the resting box has nothing to re-fill — Material's unselected error is border-only too). Dead outranks wrong: the arm sits before the disabled arm in source, losing the specificity tie on purpose, and a law pins the ordering — **including the GLYPH, corrected 2026-08-09**: the invalid arm names a destructive FAMILY token, the shared disabled arm rewrites tone ROLES and so could not reach it, and a dead invalid tick rendered at full strength (measured louder than a live sound one). The disabled arm now stands the glyph down as `--tone-contrast`, the role rather than a literal.

**Open, and the one part of this the numbers argue against (audit 2026-08-09):** the arm is written on `.kui-mark`, so it reaches the switch TRACK — which has no glyph, and that is what the design leans on everywhere else. Measured there, the on/off delta collapses from 2.981:1 light / 4.239:1 dark to **1.038:1 / 1.129:1, APCA Lc 0.0 in both**: a hue rotation at constant luminance, the same shape §8 recorded as a defect on 2026-08-04 for the invalid border. Dark still reads through the grip (14.5:1); light has nothing reaching 3:1 for on-versus-off (WCAG 1.4.11). Two options, Kushagra's call: scope the wash to the glyph-bearing members (the switch's destructive EDGE already signals at 3.97:1 / 9.43:1, and the switch already leaves the look axis on the same "a well is not a seal" argument), or give the track `--destructive-solid` (3.28 / 4.19 — Material's answer, rejected as a shout when the wash was chosen). Keeping the soft wash on a glyph-less member is what the measurement does not permit.

**Reversed 2026-08-04, and the original reasoning was wrong twice over.** The border was `--destructive-border` — step 7, which shares its lightness with every other tone at that step *by law* — so the entire validity signal was a hue rotation at constant luminance: measured against the field fill, 22.8 -> 23.9 Lc in light and 10.3 -> **9.8** in dark. Going invalid made the border *fainter* than the resting one it replaced, and at constant luminance it is close to invisible to a red-green colourblind user. And the ring stayed accent, measuring **6.4x** the visual weight of the error border beside it, so the error signal was at its faintest exactly when the user focused the field to correct it. The stated defence — that a destructive ring "would have to re-clear the APCA floor per mode" — is simply false, measured: destructive clears Lc 55-65 in both modes against a floor of 45. What actually argued for one ring was consistency with Spectrum/Radix/Primer, and that is outweighed by two chromatic signals arguing on one control. Both values are now law-tested against the Lc 45 non-text floor.

**Colour alone is not sufficient signalling (WCAG 1.4.1).** The border is the system's part; the error *text* is `Field.Error`'s, and a field that renders no message is a composition defect rather than a token one. The labelled, described, error-carrying arrangement is a block.

### Disabled: a role remap, never opacity (decided 2026-08-02)

Foreground remaps to `neutral-8`; the fill and the border come from `disabledSteps`, one home in config (ALPHA since 2026-08-17 — the dead palette predated the ramp move and its opaque step vanished exactly where a pane's lighting darkens its ground: a disabled off-switch at a dark card's pooled bottom, measured invisible; an alpha fill recedes relative to its LOCAL ground). **PER MODE since 2026-08-19, and the rule is one sentence: dead sits one ramp step under the live wells' RESTING step in its own mode** — fill `neutral-a2` light and `neutral-a3` dark, border `neutral-a2` light and `neutral-a3` dark (one value in both until 2026-08-24, when the live field edge lightened to a3 in light — the dead border sat exactly ON the live edge, a disabled field byte-identical to a live one at the boundary, and the recession rule moved it down with the edge it recedes from). The flat a3 this paragraph stated until 2026-08-20 delivered that only in dark, where the live soft trio rests at a4; in light the live trio rests at a3, so a disabled button's box was byte-identical to a live MEDIUM button's in all four painted channels and the dead state rode on label colour alone (audit 2026-08-18). The border left the opaque `neutral-6` in the same change, for the same one-sentence reason read in the other direction: against a live field's alpha edge in dark it OUT-contrasted it, making the one dead control the strongest boundary on the row. Hover and press stop firing. **Non-tone roles dim by `--disabled-dim` (70%, one config number)** — the roles minted for role semantics (`--color-thumb`, `--color-track`) have no palette row to stand down to, so they mix toward transparent by the one factor; writing that rule caught the slider's dead RAIL, which reads `--color-track` directly and had never dimmed at all (the disabled-slider-handle shape's third appearance). No `opacity` — it stacks on tinted surfaces and silently voids every generated contrast guarantee, where the remap keeps "legible but clearly off" as a designed, testable pair. Cursor drops to `--cursor-disabled` (see Cursors, above). Not focusable at rest, matching the native element — except while loading, below.

### Loading: the label never hides (decided 2026-08-02)

`data-loading` + `aria-busy`; interaction blocked; focus retained via Base UI's `focusableWhenDisabled`, so a click that flips a button to loading does not dump keyboard focus.

Composition: **a control with an icon swaps the icon for the Spinner in the same `--icon-size-N` box — zero layout shift. A text-only control gains the Spinner beside its label, and the label stays.** This reverses the label-hiding pattern (Polaris, Primer): a button that loses its label stops saying what it is doing, and the accepted cost is a slight width change on text-only controls while loading.

**Spinner is a public component and deliberately primitive (revised 2026-08-03):** one SVG of eight static spokes (twelve at first; eight halves nothing visually and trims the DOM — Kushagra's call) rotated as a whole by a `steps(8)` keyframe — the indicator *ticks* from spoke to spoke rather than sweeping, which is the native-platform idiom. The first build was the border-trick arc (one element, no SVG); it was rejected by eye, and the conic-gradient attempt at spokes failed structurally — a conic gradient cuts angular wedges, not parallel-sided bars, so the spokes are geometry in the component. Still no JS and one composited transform per frame; `fill: currentColor` inherits the label's colour with no token; sized by the icon family. Under `prefers-reduced-motion` it slows (3s) rather than stops — a busy indicator that stops moving is information lost. **Revised 2026-08-06 (LOG): the rotation rides an HTML `<span>` wrapper around the svg.** An SVG root's transform is not reliably composited — some engines animate it on the main thread, and a busy indicator that freezes when the main thread blocks fails its one job. The wrapper owns the icon box, the animation, `fill` (inherited into the svg) and the ref; the svg only fills it. The second element is sanctioned by the anatomy criterion: forced by something non-visual, not layout convenience.

### The icon box (decided 2026-08-02)

`--icon-size-1..4` = 16 / 16 / 20 / 24 fine, **20 / 20 / 24 / 24 coarse**, pulled by the size index. Sizes 1 and 2 share 16 because the icon grid the ecosystem draws on (16/20/24 — Primer, Lucide, Material) has nothing legible below it, and a 14px raster of a 24-grid icon blurs its strokes. **Icons are `ReactNode` slots; the package ships no icon dependency.** Hugeicons is the blessed set — installed by the app (and the docs), never by the library: a bundled set is dead weight for anyone with their own, an update treadmill, and a licence surface. The Spinner rides the same box.

**The box joined the pointer axis 2026-08-10, and the correction is a lesson about one sentence covering two axes.** It shipped as "NOT part of the density or pointer sets — the icon grid is a perception floor, not a breathing-room choice", which is a *density* argument, and a correct one: a compact size 2 and a comfortable size 2 are the same control at different airiness, so the glyph inside them is the same glyph. It was extended to pointer in the same breath, and pointer is not breathing room — coarse means the screen is held close and touched, which is exactly why it re-prices the control box, type steps 1–4, the line box and the whole mark family. Measured at size 2 default when Kushagra caught it in the playground: the button grows 32 → 44, its label 14 → 16, its checkbox sibling 16 → 20, and the glyph sat at 16 in **both** worlds, reading thin against everything around it. iOS scales SF Symbols with Dynamic Type for the same reason. **The stroke needed no answer of its own** — it lives inside the glyph's viewBox and scales with the box, so nothing scaled because the box did not. That is true of the LADDER and was read for three days as true of the VALUE, which is the hole the next paragraph closes: a stroke rides its own box, and two glyphs drawn on different grids ride different boxes at the same stated number.

It is its **own designed ladder per world**, not a ride on the line box the way the mark family is: an icon must land on the 16/20/24 drawing grid, and a type step would put it wherever the ramp puts it. Coarse stays on that grid rather than continuing past it — sizes 3 and 4 both land on 24, mirroring the fine world's doubled entry at the bottom. Density still declares no icon box anywhere, law-asserted. v0 for the eye pass, and the whole thing is law-tested twice: the emitted ladder (both worlds declare it in full, coarse never smaller, and a vacuity guard requiring that some size actually rose) and the **painted** width of an icon inside a mounted Button per size, which is the assertion that would have failed on the shipped code.

**The set closed 2026-08-10 in both places at once, and the WEIGHT is stated once, below — not here (corrected 2026-08-26).** apps/docs installs `@hugeicons/react` + `@hugeicons/core-free-icons` (both MIT) and sets the icon stroke once, in `app/icons.tsx`, so a Hugeicons glyph and a package-drawn caret sitting in one toolbar are the same weight by construction rather than by coincidence. The number was 1.5 from 2026-08-10 to 2026-08-23 (and 1.25 for one commit during the radius eye pass); it is `iconStroke = 1.75` since, and this paragraph carried the superseded 1.5 as a standing claim until it was struck — which is the drift a re-introduced literal would come from, and which a source law now catches.

**What the stroke means on the glass, because the number is not pixels (rewritten 2026-08-23).** A stroke is stated in VIEWBOX units, so the painted weight is `stroke x box / viewBox`. The package states it once as `iconStroke` — **1.75, for the 24-unit grid the ecosystem draws on** (Lucide, Hugeicons, Heroicons, Phosphor, Feather) — and exports it, because §8 ships no icon set and the glyphs beside ours belong to whatever set the app installed. That gives **1.17px at sizes 1-2** (16px box), 1.46px at size 3, 1.75px at size 4; on touch, 1.46px at sizes 1-2 and 1.75px at 3-4, because the box moved (2026-08-10) and the stroke rides it. A constant painted weight is not expressible here — it would need the stroke to change with the box, which CSS cannot compute per instance — so the ladder's thinner strokes at the small end are the shipped behaviour, not an oversight.

**The package's own glyphs are drawn on a 16 viewBox, and that is why one literal was two weights.** (This sentence said "seven" until 2026-08-26; Tree's chevron made it eight. The count is deliberately not restated here — §9's own remedy for the shadow-consumer number — because the source-walk law that forbids a hand-written stroke is the only home that cannot drift.) Until 2026-08-23 every glyph in the repo said `strokeWidth="1.5"` — the checkbox tick, both chevrons, the menu tick, the notice's dismiss, and the docs app's Hugeicons — and they did not match: 1.5 on a 16 grid in a 16px box paints 1.50px, 1.5 on a 24 grid paints 1.00px, so a select's chevron had been 50% heavier than the icon in the button beside it since the day the docs installed a set. Kushagra reported the thin half (*"the icons are too thin... Usually 1.75 stroke width works"*), and raising the shared literal would have WIDENED the gap. `glyphStroke` is `iconStroke` restated for 16 — a derivation, never a second judged number — and a mounted law reads the painted weight of a package glyph against a 24-grid glyph carrying the public value, in the same box, at every index. 1.75 is v1 KookieUI's own settled number, and it is taste like every other number in `config.ts` — judged by eye, one line, moved whenever the eye says so.

### Why uniform steps feel uniform

"+1 step" reads as the same perceived change across every hue **only because of the perceptually-even L ladder** (section 7). On a naive sRGB ramp, one step would read as more change on blue than on yellow, and interaction consistency would silently break per color. The color architecture is what makes uniform interaction feedback possible. The whole system is one decision wearing different clothes.

---

## 9. The component axis model

**Decision: components expose semantic axes that resolve to appearance. Appearance is always output, never input. Each axis is derived from one yes/no about the component's nature.**

This is the Apple lesson done correctly: separate *meaning* from *loudness* from *depth* from *substance*, rather than collapsing them onto one "variant" or "prominence" prop. Naive `primary | secondary | danger` is strictly **less** expressive than soft/solid, because it fuses loudness and meaning and so cannot express a low-emphasis destructive action (a quiet delete link). Apple splits role (`.destructive`) from prominence (`.bordered` / `.borderedProminent`); we mirror the structure, not the glass.

### The axes

```
tone       neutral | accent | destructive | (success | warning ...)   picks the HUE (accent is the app's one brand family, authored in config — section 7)
emphasis   loud | medium | quiet                                      how loud, three rungs
bordered   boolean                                                    containment: separate the control from a busy backdrop
elevation  DELETED 2026-08-03 — see section 10               separation is border and fill; no shadows
material   solid | thin | regular | thick                             backdrop defense — a THEME axis since 2026-08-16, never a component prop; what a component states is placement (`backdrop`)
states     rest | hover | press                                       the +1/+2 step rule (interactive only)
size       1 | 2 | 3 | 4                                               height index (controls) / padding + corner (surfaces)
```

`appearance` (the actual fill/shadow/blur) is the **resolved output** of (tone x emphasis x bordered x material), never set directly.

**There is no `variant` prop. `emphasis` is the only loudness axis**, and its three rungs are the recipes — there is no second, rawer naming underneath to escape into. The old five-name set (`solid / soft / surface / outline / ghost`) is dead: it named construction rather than loudness, and mixing metaphors is what made it unmappable to a ladder.

**Why three rungs and not four or five.** A rung must earn a *visible* step or it does not exist. Three stays obviously separable, and matches the roughly three prominence levels iOS ships. This also settles the old blocker honestly: the ladder is no longer stretched to cover recipes that were never loudness.

**Why `bordered` is separate.** Border-versus-fill is *containment*, a different question from loudness, and forcing it onto the ladder was the entire `surface` incoherence. As an orthogonal boolean it reproduces the old range with fewer concepts: `quiet + bordered` is the old outline, `medium + bordered` the old surface. Border does one job — separate the control from a busy background — which is also why it pairs naturally with material (section 10).

**`loud + bordered` is useless, and stays legal (judged 2026-08-03, Kushagra).** A step-7 border against a solid step-9 fill does no containment work — the fill already separates itself. It is not forbidden: refusing the combination would cost either an axis-multiplying CSS rule (the exact shape the additivity law forbids) or component-level prop logic, both worse than a no-op nobody reaches for. The preview stops showing the cell; documentation says don't.

**On controls, `bordered` is honestly part of the emphasis ladder (named 2026-08-04).** `medium + bordered` reads louder than `medium`; the composed cells reproduce v1's five variants exactly (ghost/outline/soft/surface/solid). The prop stays — the ladder is real and border is how two of its rungs are built — but its §-framing as pure containment was only half true, and consumers should read the pairs as loudness steps, not decoration.

### TextField is a field, and its wrapper is the control (decided 2026-08-04, Kushagra)

**`TextField` is `size × material`, two slots, and the native input's own props. No `emphasis`, no `tone`, no `bordered`.** Loudness ranks actions *against their siblings* — Save loud, Cancel quiet, side by side — and text fields never rank against each other: a form where one field is louder than the next is incoherent, and "a loud input" names nothing the system can mean. This is the Card argument arriving at a control. What the world calls input variants (Material's filled vs outlined, Radix's `classic | surface | soft`) is not per-field loudness but **app identity** — an app uses one construction throughout — so if it ever ships it is a Theme prop, the way `depth` is, and not before a real app forces it. The field is bordered by identity: on a field the border is the affordance, not a rung.

**The visible control is the WRAPPER, and that is what makes slots legitimate anatomy.** A field that can hold an icon inside its border cannot keep that border on the `<input>`. Once a wrapper owns the border it owes three things no consumer can compose from outside: clicking anywhere in the box must land the caret; the input must yield to the slots without breaking `::placeholder`, autofill or selection; and an interactive trailing control must stay reachable without stealing the field's focus. That is §10's anatomy criterion met — *system-owned only where something non-visual forces it* — which is precisely why this component has slots and Card does not. `leading` and `trailing` are props rather than compound children, so the field owns order and padding deterministically; free-form children invite the arbitrary stacking that made shadcn's card header the cautionary case.

**Consequences of the wrapper, each recorded because each is a departure:** `ref` goes to the *input* (`.focus()`, `.select()`, the value — what a caller actually holds a field for) while `className` and `style` dress the wrapper, so this is the first component where the element you style and the element you hold differ. Focus rings when the input holds focus, not on `:focus-within` (see §8). The caret cursor and text selection are re-enabled over the control skeleton, which forbids both for good reason on a button. The fill states are declared as constants — a field's fill does not move; its border and ring carry its states — and that is not decoration but a hard requirement: the shared layer swaps to `--kui-fill-src-hover` on pointer, and a field that left it unset would go transparent under the pointer, invalid at computed-value time. **The same sentence has to be said twice, once for the fill the component declares and once for the fill the layer derives** (2026-08-05): material is a fill *modifier* with an interaction ramp of its own, so pinning the three sources to one colour still left a glass field lightening under the pointer — 64% -> 72% -> 80% of the same white at the middle thickness — and the trailing button fired it just by being crossed on the way to the caret. The derived hover and active fills are pinned to the resting derived value, which names no thickness.

**`disabled` is read off the input, not only stamped from the prop** (2026-08-05). Inside a `Field.Root disabled` the flag never passes through this component: Base UI computes `fieldDisabled || disabledProp` at the input, so the wrapper — the element that paints — was never told, and a disabled fieldset rendered a field that looked entirely live. The shared layer now carries a direct-child `:has()` arm beside the attribute, exactly the way `invalid` has always been read. Direct child only: a disabled control hosted in a slot must not grey out the field containing it.

**`children` is not part of the API.** It was typed on the props and spread onto the `<input>`, so `<TextField>x</TextField>` type-checked and threw at render. The slots are the way in.

**`type` is a closed union, the way `size` is** (2026-08-05): `text | email | password | search | tel | url | number`. On the native element `type` is not one axis but a *component selector* — `hidden` renders no box, `checkbox`/`radio`/`range`/`color`/`file` each render a different control with its own anatomy, and `submit`/`button` are buttons. Unconstrained, `type="hidden"` produced a **visible empty bordered box**, which is the argument in one render: the wrapper is what draws the border and the height, and a wrapper cannot honour a type it was never told about. Date and time are absent on purpose — they render a native picker inside the box and are a component, not a value of this one.

**The value wears content dress, not control dress** (2026-08-05, Kushagra: "too thick, and placeholder not light enough"). The skeleton's medium weight was designed for button labels and reached the input through `font: inherit` — inherited, never decided. A field's value is the user's *content*, and content in this system rests regular (§15, Text's own default); the field now declares it, slots included, and a hosted control re-declares its own medium through the skeleton. The placeholder was the same finding one layer down, and there it was outright drift: it read `--color-text-muted` while the faint role's own definition (§15) named "a placeholder" as its case. It moved to faint — and back to **muted** on 2026-08-10, when the ink ladder became solved targets and faint became the exception rung below the reading floor. The round trip is not indecision: faint's definition changed under it, and a placeholder is information said quietly, not something stood down.

**`readOnly` is a state with a resolved appearance** (2026-08-05). It shipped fully accepted and resolving to nothing at all: keystrokes were refused while the field looked pixel-identical to the one beside it, so the only feedback was typing and having nothing happen. It is **not** disabled and must not borrow that vocabulary — the value is live, selectable, focusable, in the tab order and submitted with the form. Exactly one thing is gone, the invitation to type, so exactly one thing changes: **the seal**, which is what makes a field read as a well you put a caret into. Border, text contrast and the caret cursor all stay. Under a material it resolves to transparent, which is the same answer the bare rung gets under glass.

**Slot content DESCRIBES the field.** Each rendered slot gets an id and is appended to the input's `aria-describedby` — appended, never replacing, since a field inside a `Field.Root` already carries a description id. Described rather than labelled is the right relationship: an adornment ("$", "USD") qualifies the value, it does not name the field, and the name is the caller's. Before this, adornments were announced — if at all — as loose text with no relationship to the field beside them.

**There is no `render` escape, and that is a decision rather than the omission it looked like** (2026-08-05). Everywhere else `render` swaps the one element that *is* the component. Here there are two and neither can move: the wrapper exists to hold a border the input cannot hold once a slot sits inside it, and the input must stay an `<input>` or the platform wiring this component exists to preserve — labelling, autofill, form association, the `type` behaviours — goes with it. `render` would have to silently mean one of them. Refused by the type, which is the law.

**What Base UI owns and we do not:** label, description and error are `Field` parts, which already do the `aria-*` wiring; the state vocabulary (`data-invalid`, `data-focused`, `data-filled`, `data-dirty`, `data-touched`) is Base UI's, read by our stylesheet. The labelled arrangement around a field is a block.

**Measured: +247 bytes gzipped for the entire second control** (9,574 -> 9,821; the same number §14 records), because the size index, the states, the disabled and invalid remaps and material all arrived from the layer Button already paid for. That is §2's additivity claim getting its first real test rather than its first assertion.

### Card is a shell (decided 2026-08-04, Kushagra)

**Card is `size` (+ the `backdrop` placement fact, §10) and children. No tone, no emphasis, no bordered, no anatomy slots. One treatment: the opaque seal (`--color-surface`), the surface radius, the padding rhythm — fixed identity, written by the component as constant data attributes, never chosen at a call site. Its BORDER left the identity 2026-08-17 (the lab port): `outlined`'s surface border is `transparent` at standard contrast, because the lab's pane is borderless — its edge is LIGHT (the cast and seat-line pool on solid, the ring on glass), never a pigment hairline. `contrast="high"` re-declares the role to `initial` and the tone system's edge returns, which is where conformance always lived (the 2026-08-07 split).**

Three arguments closed it, in order:

1. **Emphasis ranks actions; a container is not an action.** A "loud card" carries no meaning the system can name, so consumers would use it as a purely visual tool — the failure a semantic system exists to prevent. The rungs stay in the surface layer for the components where tone genuinely means something: Notice (status — the three names this sentence used to list collapsed into it 2026-08-21, §29), and interactive surfaces (the control ladder).
2. **Anatomy is system-owned only where something non-visual forces it.** Dialog's title/description are forced by a11y wiring (`aria-labelledby`/`aria-describedby`); Notice's by status semantics, and Field's by three contracts at once (§28). Nothing forces a Card layout: title-then-description-then-actions is one layout among many (stat cards, media cards, profile cards), and blessing one deprecates the rest. **Layouts are blocks (kookie-blocks), not components.** The considered-and-cut slot set is recorded in LOG so it is not re-litigated: Header (a wrapper with no layout job once the corner slot died), Footer (positional name, and Apple's `Section(footer:)` means fine print — the semantic name was Actions), Action-in-header (its only legitimate tenant is card-object operations — dismiss, overflow — whose components do not exist yet; shipped early it gets squatted by task actions, which is shadcn's own demo failure).
3. **Material stays, because a Card is a surface** and surfaces defend their backdrops — cards land on hostile imagery routinely. Apple's model concurs: no Card component anywhere, `GroupBox`/inset `Section` with one fixed treatment, materials as the real axis, card-ness composed rather than shipped.

**A pane holds what it contains, and a child may reach its edge (2026-08-20).** Two halves, shipped together, neither complete alone. The surface layer sets `overflow: clip`: a pane's corner is its SHAPE — since 2026-08-17 a squircle — so what it holds obeys it. Without this a picture reaching the edge squared off the corner it sat in and the pane stopped reading as one object. Every peer clips (Mantine, MUI, Ant, Chakra); this system did not, and had never decided not to. `clip` and not `hidden`, on the argument the flight rules already won: a `hidden` box is a scroll container — scrollable by script and by the browser's own scroll-into-view, just without a scrollbar — which is how a select's panel came to slide its own contents under a growing frame. `clip` takes no offset and unwinds none, and it leaves a sticky child inside a card sticking to the real scrollport.

The other half is `bleed` (section 3), the child's own statement that it reaches the edge — **and since 2026-08-29 the keyword works on the padding rows too, with the sign flipped**: a margin's `bleed` cancels the enclosing surface's padding, a padding's re-applies it, so a bled region can hand the pane's own air back to its content. `<Tabs mx="bleed">` runs a tab bar to the pane's edges and `px="bleed"` on the panel puts its text exactly where the pane's padding would have — the pair ScrollArea has always performed internally (bleed to the surface's edge, re-pad the viewport), handed to call sites. One keyword because it is one mechanism seen from its two halves; the same explicit `0px` fallback, so outside any surface the pair is an honest no-op. Gap still refuses it — a gap has no relation to the pane's inset. The sentence that kept it out ("padding rejects a negative length") was true of the negative calc and silent about the positive one, and the law that pinned the pass-through failed on the fix, as a law encoding a superseded decision should. **Together they are the media card without a media slot** — the layout every peer solved with a component part, built here out of a value and a shape. What clipping costs: an element that deliberately hangs off a pane's corner — a chip, an avatar overlapping the edge — cannot, and the escape is `style={{ overflow: "visible" }}` at the call site, where opting out of the shape is visible in review. What it OBLIGES: a clipping pane must pad at least the focus ring's reach, or a control resting against the inside of the padding loses the outer edge of its ring. The surface band clears it in every cell (the tightest is compact size 1 at 8px against a 4px reach) and the menu's panel did not — which is why `--kui-menu-pad` is a `max()` against the ring's own tokens rather than a number. Law-checked across every size × density.

**A scroll region inside a pane: the box is the pane's, the padding is the content's (2026-08-20, Kushagra).** A pane's padding and a scroller's box want opposite things. Pad the pane and inset the scroller — which is what a call site naturally writes, and what this repo's own preview shipped — and every row that leaves the box is sliced against an invisible line with empty padding beneath it, while the bar floats somewhere inside the pane instead of hanging on its edge. The resolution is the one **the menu found first and then kept to itself**: the scroller runs to the pane's own edges, so a row leaves under the corner and the bar sits where every platform puts it, and the padding moves INSIDE the viewport, where it scrolls with the content and the first and last rows still sit in from the edges at rest.

It is the shared surface layer's, automatic, and keyed on being a **direct child** of the pane. That keeps it away from a scroller inside a Surface inside a card, which answers to its own container. It does NOT keep it away from a pane that is itself a row — the claim that "two scrollers side by side in a Flex are not the pane's children" is true when the Flex is nested and false when the card IS the Flex, and the audit measured the cost: two scrollers overlapping by 36px, with a point inside the left one's rows hit-testing to the right one. **A row or grid pane holding a scroller is not supported**, stated rather than detected: a style query on layout's `--kui-fd` was built and measured working, then backed out because §2's stem law forbids one layer from even mentioning a name layout declares; a `.kui-stack` class was refused because Stack is `<Box display="flex" direction="column">` with zero CSS of its own, so a hand-written Box meaning the same thing would be excluded for spelling. Nothing in the package or the docs writes one. **Which block edges bleed is asked of the DOM, never of a prop**: `:first-child` and `:last-child`, so a scroller with a heading above it reclaims nothing there and its neighbour's gap survives; the viewport's padding mirrors the same two questions, because a side that did not bleed must not pad. `flex: 1` is declared unconditionally — inert in a block pane, the whole plumbing in a column one — and a pane whose ONLY child is a scroller becomes a flex COLUMN itself. That arm needs no assumption about direction (an only child touches all four edges whatever the axis) and it does overrule a stated `render={<Flex/>}`, deliberately: with one child there is nothing to lay out beside it, and the column is what lets it take the pane's height. This clause said for one day that a row and a column are the same layout with a single item — they are not, and the defect that killed it is recorded in LOG: as a row item `flex: 1` sizes the WIDTH while `min-inline-size: auto` floors it at the content's min-content, so a wide list made the scroller as wide as its content and both scrollbars vanished. So a card that IS a list states a height and nothing else.

**A pane with NEIGHBOURS supplies its own column, unless the call site stated a layout (2026-08-21).** For one day this clause ended "and a header/list/footer panel adds only `render={<Stack gap/>}`", which was a workaround written down as a design. `flex: 1` is inert in a block container, so the most ordinary composition the library has — a card with a height, holding a heading, a list and an action — computed a 1440px viewport inside a 300px pane, never became a scroll container, drew no bar, and had its action button removed from the page by the `overflow: clip` this same rule obliges. The head comment in surfaces.css had NAMED the gap ("what is unguarded is only the sibling case") without closing it, and every sibling law in the suite mounted `render={<Stack/>}` — already a column — so the fixture could not tell a working rule from a dead one. **The system now supplies the column wherever nobody stated a layout**, `:not(.kui-box)` being that question: a pane that is a Box said what it wanted, so a column already works and a row or grid stays the unsupported arrangement above rather than being silently restated as something else. It is not a spelling test — a hand-written `<Box display="flex">` card is excluded because it chose, exactly as a `<Flex>` one is.

**And the rule reaches through exactly one box: `.kui-dialog-body` (2026-08-21).** That div exists only so the entry can blur the content (§24) and it stands between the pane and the caller's children, so every selector here — each of which asks about a DIRECT child — stopped at it: no ScrollArea in a dialog had ever bled, and none had ever become a scroll container, whatever height the call site stated (measured: a 400px panel holding a 1440px viewport with its Save button at y=1705). The body is now a column when it holds a scroller and a plain block when it does not, and the six member rules read `:is(.kui-surface, .kui-dialog-body)`. Named rather than generalised, because it is the only such box in the library: Menu and Select put their scroller OUTSIDE the flying body, and AlertDialog owns its content and holds no scroller at all.

Rejected: **leaving it to composition.** It works — measured, four inline declarations and a private token name at every call site — and that is the objection: a system whose ordinary compositions are reachable only by hand-plumbing has not shipped them. **The height stays the call site's**, which is not the same concession: a pane's extent is the app's, the same class of value as the page's colour, and a pane with no height simply grows and pays nothing for this rule. Menu's private spelling (`padding: 0` on the popup, the pad restated on its viewport) was **deleted onto the shared rule** in the same change — same pixels, judged by the menu's own untouched laws, one home for the fact.

**A card can be CHOSEN, and a card can be DEAD (2026-08-22, Kushagra).** Two states an interactive card had never had, closed together because they are the same omission: §10 says a card that is a button reuses the control state machine, and the state machine had reached its colours (2026-08-03) and its motion (2026-08-17) but never its states.

**Dead first, because it was measured, not argued.** A `<Card render={<button disabled/>}>` computed BYTE-IDENTICAL to a live one — same seal, same ink, `cursor: pointer` — so a dead card still promised a press. The shared disabled remap names this selector now (one fact, one home: what "disabled" means), which brings the cursor and the tone roles. Two things it cannot bring, and the parallel with the mark family is exact: the resting fill is not a tone role, it is the seal (audit D6), and neither is the tone-less foreground vocabulary a card's words read — so the fill, the ink trio and the cast are stated in surfaces.css. Four changes, which is what a disabled Button shows: the fill recedes to the dead step, the words dim, the cursor stops promising, and the cast goes. **The cast is a §5 statement rather than a dimming** — elevation says a thing sits above the plane and can be picked up, and a dead card cannot be. The motion stands down on the same arm, which needs no `:hover`/`:active` blocks of its own: the arm ties with them at (0,2,0) and sits after them, so it wins on source order. **The first cut wrote those blocks anyway and a sabotage pass appeared to prove them unreachable — which was wrong about the hover one and cost a same-day repair (Kushagra: "why does a disabled card respond to hover?").** The MOTION needs no arms: the disabled rule ties with hover and press at (0,2,0) and sits after them, so it wins on source order. The PAINT does: the hover and press rules set `background-color` directly, and a direct declaration beats a custom-property indirection, so re-pointing `--kui-sf-fill-src` never reaches them. The pass was green because the only law watching read travel and scale — the axis that was already right. **A sabotage that survives is evidence about the LAW as often as about the code.** The press arm survives a second pass honestly: with a mouse a press always co-occurs with a hover, so the hover arm covers it; on touch there is no hover rule and it is the only defence. No mounted law can reach that (Chromium does not emulate the `hover` feature), so a node law holds it and claims only that the declaration is stated.

**Chosen adds NO API, and that is the decision rather than an economy.** Apple has two patterns and neither is a coloured card: an accent RING around a picture (wallpapers, appearance thumbnails), or a CHECKMARK at the end of a text row. Material 3 adds a tonal fill, which is the one this system cannot take — a card has no tone (§11), and a filled card claims a rank it has not earned. This system had already answered "pick one of several": it is a radio group (§26, the segmented control — *"never an aria attribute bolted onto a Button"*). So a selectable card is a card that IS the label of a real radio — `<RadioGroup><Card render={<label/>}><Radio value=…/>…</Card></RadioGroup>`. The semantics, the keyboard, the form value and the announcement are the primitive's; the whole card is pressable because that is what a label does; the visible mark is the Radio the composition placed. **So Apple's checkmark is the call site's to position and the EDGE is the system's**, which keeps Card's refusal of anatomy intact. It reads `[data-checked]` and `[data-selected]` — §23's shared vocabulary — so a board of checkboxes lights the same way with no second rule, and it is scoped to a label that HOLDS a control, so a card containing an ordinary ticked setting is not "chosen".

**Rejected:** a `selected` prop on Card (a second way to say what the radio already says, with nothing keeping the two in agreement); `render={<Radio/>}` (in this package `render` means "keep my dress, change my element", so a Radio that shed the mark family on sight would contradict every other use of it); and a hand-rolled `role="radio" aria-checked` on a button, which is the shape §26 refused by name.

**Two roles were minted, because a rung in the surface layer may not name a tone family** — the law that made both omissions visible. `--disabled-ink` is the dead text colour, which recipes.css had written by hand five times and surfaces.css could not write at all; both layers read it now. `--selected-edge` is the chosen signal, and it is deliberately NOT `--focus-ring` even though the two resolve to the same pigment today: being chosen and being focused are different statements and must stay separately correctable. **The interactive recipe widened to `label:has(.kui-control)`** — a label's whole job is to activate the control it holds, so pressing one IS pressing that control; scoped to a label that holds one, because a card labelling something outside itself is not a thing you press. `:where()` contributes zero specificity, so the recipe stayed at (0,1,0) and every stand-down weighed against it still holds. The edge is v0 and the tint was deliberately NOT built: the eye pass decides whether one hairline is enough at card scale.

### Surface is a ground (decided 2026-08-20, Kushagra)

**A Card is an object. A Surface is a ground.** An object seals, catches light and casts; a ground is what an object sits on. Two shapes, one statement: a bounded region of a page that holds cards, and a bed inside a card that holds something quieter — a code block, a settings group. They are one component rather than two because they are the same sentence; had they needed two looks they would have needed props, and props are what would have made this an escape hatch instead of a component.

**The gap it closes.** The system had no way to make a surface at all: every surface was a library component with a pinned identity, so an app or a block needing a region we did not ship hand-painted a div — a raw neutral, a guessed radius, a hairline — at every call site. The builder's own canvas is the evidence, and it got the corner **backwards**: a size-2 corner around size-3 cards. A container has to out-round what it holds.

**It takes no axis, and the absence is the design.** No fill, no edge toggle, no tone, no emphasis, no material, no shadow. The ground and the hairline are the component. This is protection, not minimalism: with a `fill`/`edge` vocabulary the first reachable combination is a seal plus a hairline — the outlined card deleted the day before — available again from every call site. **The failure a system cannot recover from is not somebody building something different; it is somebody building the same thing a second way, which then drifts out of reach of a fix.** A ground cannot pass for a card, so it cannot start that.

**Why the hairline is not an emphasis step**, which Button's `bordered` invites as a question: there the border ranks (quiet < quiet+border < medium) and the three rungs say different things about how loud an action is. Two grounds, one lined and one not, say exactly the same thing — the test an axis must pass, and the one `surfaceLook` failed on 2026-08-20. (This paragraph used to close with "and it is also load-bearing in dark, where the ground and the page are the same colour". That was true only while the dark ground had collapsed onto the page, and the next two paragraphs record it stopping being true on 2026-08-21 — the retracted clause stood here for five days beside its own correction, 2026-08-26 audit. The argument above is what carries the refusal; the clause never did.)

**The ground is an absolute pair, not a relative step, and that was measured.** The elegant design is an alpha one step under whatever is behind it: one value, adapts anywhere, flips direction by mode for free. It is wrong in dark. Dark's alpha ramp is built from white, so a ground over the page (`--neutral-1`, #0f0f10) lands near #161617 while the card it holds is `--neutral-2` (#141516) — the cards darker than the ground holding them, the nesting inverted in the mode where it is hardest to see. The platforms disagree on direction and both were checked — Apple's ladders go *lighter* as you nest deeper in dark (#000 → #1C1C1E → #2C2C2E), GitHub's `canvas.inset` goes *darker* than `canvas.default`. Darker-in-both was taken first, because it makes the word mean one thing.

**It was unreachable in dark, and it shipped as a collapse (corrected 2026-08-21, Kushagra, judged on screen).** The page is `--neutral-1`, the last rung there is, so there was nothing below it to step to and the ground was set equal to the page — stated at the time as an accepted consequence ("a ground is page-level and the cards are what lift"). Light hides the problem because the card's fill is pure white, which is not on the grey ramp at all: a free step that lets the page sit on rung 1 and still leave rung 2 underneath for a ground. Dark has no equivalent — the card cannot be "pure black", so it takes rung 2, the page takes rung 1, and there is no rung 0.

**So a ground steps OFF the page, away from whichever extreme that mode's page sits against**: down from near-white in light, up from near-black in dark. Dark's value is a literal between the two rungs (midway between `#0f0f10` and `#141516`), because there is no rung there — that is the finding, not a workaround. Light is untouched; it already had room and it already read. The two modes differ in direction and that is not an inconsistency to tidy away: a page sits at an extreme of its own ramp in both, and the only direction that exists is away from it.

**Its size join reads the OVERLAY band**, and the sharing is the point: a pane that contains panes must out-round its contents, which Dialog established on 2026-08-10 (an overlay wears the corner of the card one size up, at every level) with the padding following on 2026-08-17. A ground holds cards for the same reason a dialog does, so it takes the same relationship rather than a second copy of it. Recorded and not fixed here: with two unrelated consumers the band's NAME is now wrong — it is not "the overlay band", it is the band a container of panes wears.

**It opens no material scope.** Glass defends a pane against something passing behind it, and a ground's backdrop is its own parent. Inside a glass card it participates in the scope already there and opens none of its own, so a card inside a Surface inside a glass region is byte-identical to the card without the Surface.

**Still open:** what `contrast="high"` does to it, whether it wants the inset shadow row or fill alone (the lab's fill-first direction says start with fill), and whether nesting one inside another is allowed — the same argument that discourages card-in-card says no.

### The page has a name (2026-08-20)

`--color-page`, the mode's brightest ground — in light the seal's own white since 2026-08-25 (derived from `surfaceColor.light.rest` in config, one home, because `--neutral-1`'s #fcfcfc read dull; a resting card on the page now separates by cast or hairline, never by fill — Apple's light mode, and the collapse dark already accepted for ground-on-page), in dark `--neutral-1`, the palette's darkest step. In each mode there is nowhere further to go, which is what makes it a page. The library has always presupposed one (the seal's comment calls a card "paper above the page") and never named it, so an app painting its background reached past the roles into the raw palette. apps/docs did exactly that, in the file that judges every other value by eye; it now paints the role, and resolves to the same pixels, which is the check on whether the name was honest.

**It is a NAME, not a mechanism** — the same class as `--shadow-1..5` (§13). The library still paints nothing: the page is the app's call, and a Theme that painted a background would repaint the page at every nested appearance scope. A law walks every stylesheet and asserts none of them reads it, with the ground held to exactly one consumer beside it.

**A ground carries no PANE lighting (2026-08-21) — and since 2026-08-26 it carries its own.** Every `.kui-surface` wears a rim from the base rule — grain and a top-down sheen — and a ground was wearing one by inheritance. It should not: the rim's own argument is that a pane catches light and that glass needs tooth, and a ground is a bed rather than an object and is never glass. The cost was measured rather than argued. The grain is a fixed 4.5% white overlay, so what it does depends entirely on what is under it: it lifts a light ground by 0.002 and a dark one by 0.042 — the same token, 28× the effect — against tonal steps of 0.011. The texture was louder than the whole ladder it sat in, which is why the fill kept reading wrong while the fill was not the problem. (The sheen is already stated per mode, 30 light / 10 dark; nobody had noticed the same asymmetry one layer down.) It is stood down on the PROPERTY, never through `--kui-sf-light`: that hook is not registered `inherits: false`, so standing it down there reached every pane inside the ground and measurably stripped the card sitting on it — the var-inheritance trap this layer has now been bitten by five times.

**Amended 2026-08-26 (Kushagra: in a glass app a Surface reads as if it came from a different system). The 2026-08-21 stand-down was right about the ingredient and one recipe too wide.** Deleting the grain was correct and stays; `background-image: none` deleted the concept with it, and left the one opaque box in the library with no light on it at all — while every pane around it grew a ring, a rim and a pool through the lab port. The question that surfaced it was whether the glass RING should reach a ground and every other plain hairline, and the answer to that is no, for a reason worth keeping: **a hairline is worth its weakest arc, and the ring has no weak arc problem only because a glass pane has four other cues covering it.** Measured on a mounted ground against the page, the ring's arcs run 0.0016–0.1780 in light (its bright arc is its TOP, which is the one edge a dent must not brighten) and 0.0482–0.3269 in dark, against a pigment hairline that holds 0.1497 and 0.2042 uniformly all the way round; under `contrast="high"` the pigment edge is 0.3863 and the ring's *best* arc is less than half of it. It also does not generalise — the ring is a corner-wrapping device (that is why it is conic), and most plain hairlines in this system are straight single lines: Separator, Blockquote's rule, the Tabs bar, the Shell seams. Converting two of eight would have produced the inconsistency it was meant to remove.

**So the ground is lit as WHAT IT IS, which is the half a pane's recipe cannot supply.** A pane is an object: light lands on its top and its underside falls away — the direction the ring and the seal's sheen both state. A ground is a recess, a hole in a plane, which is why it casts nothing two paragraphs up; the same light is blocked by the wall at its top and reaches the floor at its bottom. That is the pane's model **inverted, never borrowed** — lighting a dent from the top makes it read as if it stuck out, which is the ring's own mistake arriving by a different road. No grain: that measurement has not changed and a bed has no tooth.

**It LIGHTENS and never darkens, and that is a same-day reversal (2026-08-26, Kushagra: "I wanted the recess to be communicated by not darkening the top band ... keep that darker tone as what it is right now, but lighten up the rest").** The first cut painted the wall as a black wash over the fill. It reads as a band laid ON the box rather than as the box being recessed, and it is loudest exactly where the box is small: the ramp is a fixed distance, so on the docs' 130px code well it covered 11% of the box against 2% on a page-sized ground — the fraction wall, on the first day of a value that was written to be fixed *because* a dent's depth is physical. So there is no dark layer at all. The ground's own colour IS the wall, showing at the top edge where nothing is laid over it, and ONE white wash lifts the floor below. `--color-ground` is deliberately not moved to do it: since the 2026-08-26 audit that value is a bed in the high-contrast edge solve, and baking a wall into it would move a conformance input to buy a taste change. The wash is where taste belongs.

**What that costs is stated, because it bounds the values.** Lightening-only has very little headroom in either mode, and the two modes run out of it for different reasons. In LIGHT the page is pure white and the ground sits 0.033 under it, so a big lift dissolves the region into the page and only its hairline says it is there. In DARK the ground sits about 0.011 UNDER the card standing on it, and lifting past that puts the cards darker than the ground holding them — the nesting inverted, which is this section's own reason for the ground being an absolute pair. **The dark dent is therefore very faint by construction** (a 0.5% lift, against light's 40%), and that is a consequence of the direction rather than a tuning miss; making it deeper means moving the card or the ground, which is a different decision.

**The law the 2026-08-21 finding never had.** That finding had to be reported by eye, because nothing asserted the lighting was quieter than the structure — the grain was not wrong for being white, it was wrong for moving a dark ground four times further than the whole page–ground–card ladder. The lift is held under one tonal step of the ladder it sits in, stated against the ladder rather than as an alpha ceiling: light's ladder is 0.033 and dark's is 0.011, three times apart, so a single ceiling would be wrong in one mode. A second law holds the lit FLOOR clear of both beds, and it exists because a sabotage pass walked through the first one — in light the ladder's ceiling IS the distance to the page, so "moved < step" happily permits arriving at it, and a 95% lift (floor 0.9984 against a 1.0 page) passed all fourteen laws. It reads the FRACTION of each gap still unspent, signed, never a distance: the first spelling of that law used an absolute value, and a floor lifted clean PAST the card satisfied "far from the card" — the exact failure it exists to catch. A ratio catches crossing (it goes negative) and dissolving (it drops under a half) with one number, and the sign carries the fact that the page sits above the ground in light and below it in dark with no per-mode arithmetic. The values (light 40%, dark 0.5%) are taste, one config line each, judged in the playground beside a ground with the recipe stood down.

**One role, not the two the plan carried.** Apple splits the page in two — `systemBackground` and `systemGroupedBackground`, alternating in light and identical in dark — and the second one collapsed the moment `--color-ground` existed: a page made of cards is exactly what a ground is. So an app wanting the Settings look paints `--color-ground`, and the pair reads as what it is: **content sits on the page, cards sit on the ground.**

### Card and its shell

**The rule the footer discussion produced, kept as the documented pattern:** a composition has exactly one task-action zone. Verbs of the task (Save, Login, Export) live together at the end; verbs on the surface itself (dismiss, overflow) are a different kind and get their slot when Dialog forces it — which it did, and Notice makes the distinction structural: its resolving action and its dismissal are two different kinds of verb on one surface (§29). The corrected login composition becomes the first documented block.

### Card-as-button: the element brings the interactivity (decided 2026-08-04)

`<Card render={<button/>}>` (or an anchor) IS the pattern — no prop, no component. The surface layer keys on the element semantics (`.kui-surface:where(button, a)`) and applies the control state machine, per this section's own rule: reuse it, never invent a surface one. Rest is pixel-identical to a plain Card — a card is a card until you point at it. Hover washes the seal to `--color-surface-hover` (guarded by `(hover: hover)`), press steps to `--color-surface-active` instantly and unguarded, keyboard gets the one shared ring, cursor and touch hygiene match the controls. **All three rungs are configured per mode**, which was wrong until 2026-08-03: hover and active were hard-coded to `--neutral-2` and `--neutral-3` for both modes while the dark seal *was* `--neutral-2`, so in dark, rest and hover were the same token and an interactive card had no hover feedback at all. Dark now rests at 2 and steps 3 / 4; light is unchanged. The law reads three resolved colours through a mounted Card, not three token names in the stylesheet. This respects the anatomy criterion: here interactivity is forced by something non-visual — the element itself — and HTML enforces the one-action rule for free, since a button cannot nest a button.

### The shadow palette: a resource, never an axis (decided 2026-08-04; consumer corrected the same day; redesigned and widened to five rows 2026-08-07)

Elevation stays deleted; taste does not. `--shadow-1..5` ships in the public token contract (§13) — five rows on the system's one index shape, ordered by height, mode-aware (dark keeps the same geometry and raises alpha only: the light source does not move at night). Row 1 is the inset well; **row 2 is the control drop** (added 2026-08-07 — the palette had no rung at button scale, and Kushagra's refutation killed the alternative: a bespoke shadow hidden inside a chrome role is a second source of shadow truth, and a block wanting a small raised chip could never reach it. More rows, not more sources); rows 3-5 are the old 2-4 renumbered while nothing is published. **Every drop row is one anatomy at a different height** (redesigned 2026-08-07, the four-worlds frame; **re-cut 2026-08-16 when the material lab's depth was adopted whole** — this paragraph described the two-layer shape until 2026-08-20): a CONTACT line — small offset, near-zero blur, the crisp dark line under the edge that makes a shadow read sharp, and constant across the ladder because contact does not change with height — plus a DROP, the body of the shadow, plus a BLAST, the far wide reach (up to 32px/80px at the top rung) that is most of what the eye reads as depth and the layer the old palette simply did not have. Both reaching layers carry **negative spread, the sharpness mechanism**: they pull the shadow in under the element so depth drops below instead of haloing sideways; the contact does not pull in, or a bright seam opens between the bottom edge and its own shadow. A law asserts the anatomy per row per mode: three layers — **row 4 is two, the lab's solid card verbatim, because two stacked drops read darker than one at matched alphas** — x always 0, the contact never pulling in, and each row's reach strictly growing.

**Consumers, exactly two, and no component API anywhere:**
- **Theme `depth="elevated"`** — the one thing that turns depth ON (§5); four rules read it. Each world scope declares the chrome roles, and the elements that read them paint them: `.kui-surface` reads `--kui-surface-chrome`, and since 2026-08-07 the control layer reads `--kui-control-chrome` + `--kui-control-light` through the emphasis ladder (below). **Fields left the set 2026-08-06 and rejoined it at control scale 2026-08-07** (the third flip, below) (they had joined 2026-08-04 on the sentence "a text field is a bordered box sitting on the page — it lifts with the cards"; the sentence was asserted, not judged): elevation separates a plane from what is BEHIND it, and a field is a **well** — content of a plane, not a plane above one. A recessed thing cannot cast a drop shadow; Material's fields are filled or outlined, never raised, and no platform shades an input. **The default is `elevated` since 2026-08-17** (the lab port: light is the world's resting state, and the playground was judged that way for a day before the default admitted it), and `flat` resolves `0 0 0 0 transparent` — a no-op LAYER, not the keyword, because `none` is illegal inside a shadow list and the pool (§10) joined the cast in one list. Flat is still byte-inert on screen; it is spelled as a layer so the list survives it. **The count that matters is not how many rules exist but that every box-shadow resolves a world chrome role** — a law walks every shipped stylesheet and asserts exactly that, and the consumer SET moved 2026-08-17 rather than merely counting up: the surface layer and the control layer's LOUD rung read the world chromes; **the field family left elevation (the fourth flip, below) and the medium rung left with it** — depth separates the focal and the floating, and a medium fill is a wash ON the plane (measured beside loud in the playground: the two floated identically and rank stopped reading); and the grips and the key cap now read their OWN designed cap-scale roles (`--grip-cast`, `--kbd-relief`) instead of the palette's control row, because the lab port made `--control-chrome` the lit BUTTON chrome — an 8px/20px blast plus an inset under-shade, priced for a 32-44px box, which read swollen under a 16-28px cap. A new consumer appearing is still a decision, and still fails in the law first. (The count is stated once, here: it read two in this paragraph and three a paragraph later while the law asserted four, which is how a number nobody can check drifts three ways — audit 2026-08-07. It then drifted again the moment the fifth consumer shipped, because Switch raised the law and the paragraph below it and left this sentence saying four — audit 2026-08-08. The lesson the second time is that a number stated once is still stated by hand: the paragraph below now points here rather than restating it.). It escapes by declaration, like every other axis: written as a descendant selector with no reset (until 2026-08-03) a nested `depth="flat"` matched nothing and the elevated ancestor's rule still reached the inner cards, so a flat panel inside an elevated shell was unbuildable. An app identity, never a per-card knob.

  **The membership sentence split by what light does (§5 amended 2026-08-07, the four-worlds frame — deliberately reversing 2026-08-06's "a button stays flat" negative law).** The PLANE criterion governs surface-scale depth: elevation dresses boxes that establish a plane of their own, and a mark still does not qualify; the FIELD rejoined at CONTROL scale 2026-08-07 (the third flip) **and left again 2026-08-17 (the FOURTH flip, Kushagra: "why should medium emphasis or text area or text field elevate at all?")**: the third flip was priced on the bordered-box identity, where a field was a raised control like its button; the fill-first identity (§19) makes it a WELL again — carved into the plane, not raised off it — and this time control-scale light exists and the answer is still no, which is what makes the fourth flip a closure rather than another oscillation. On glass a field keeps the POOL (matter, §10) and sheds the transmitted cast (elevation). The press-tighten rule re-keyed to `[data-emphasis="loud"]` with it, so a pressed medium button cannot resurrect a shadow its rest no longer owns. But in a world with a light source a raised CONTROL is lit too: a solid-fill rung casts the palette's control row (`--kui-control-chrome`, one shared shadow — no button can have a different one, because no button owns one) and catches light on top (`--kui-control-light`, gradient layers painted over whatever fill the rung chose, tone-independent by construction). The wiring is the rung's own statement — `--kui-ct-cast`/`--kui-ct-light` in the emphasis ladder: loud maps both world tokens, medium maps the cast only (a white wash over a pastel fill reads as fog — judged three times in the preview, 2026-08-07; gradient light is for saturated solid fills, and a pale button carries shadow alone), quiet maps `none` for both (nothing to light, and declared rather than omitted so a quiet control inside a louder surface cannot inherit the ancestor's cast). The light never moves with state; disabled stands it down (no promise, no hover above the page); material stands the CATCH down (a gradient on a veil repaints the material's own face) and keeps the cast. **A checked mark catches too (same day, Kushagra):** the checked fill is the mark family's LOUD rung (§11 — state is the mark's emphasis), so under an elevated world a checked checkbox or selected radio wears exactly the loud button's gradient over the same accent solid — one material for a form's solid fills — and never a cast: a mark is a well that fills, not a raised key. The mark declares its light `none` at rest (declared, not omitted — the ladder's tokens inherit, and a mark under a loud-emphasis ancestor would otherwise wear its light; law-mounted with a bare `data-emphasis="loud"` div). Button and the checked marks today. **And the slider thumb casts ALWAYS — flat world included (same day, Kushagra: "slider thumb needs elevation always, it's how it should be").** Depth as role semantics, the kill switch's second named exception beside the circle (§6): a grip that does not sit above its rail stops reading as a grip, and every platform ships the handle shadowed in every context. Mechanically it reads a role's VALUE rather than the world switch, which is what "always" means — the flat world cannot strip it. **One-lift-per-pane CAN, since 2026-08-24 (Kushagra's glass model): "always" is about the WORLD, and glass is a MATERIAL, the axis the sentence was never about** — a grip in a pane is a ridge in the glass, not a pebble on it, and Apple's liquid glass ships the handle flat, retiring the "every platform" clause that stood here. The stand-down arrives through the `--kui-grip-cast` hook (the keycap's own mechanism), set by the pane rule in surfaces.css and consulted before `--grip-cast` in all three grip sheets; law-mounted per grip with a solid-ground negative control. **The role became the grips' OWN 2026-08-17 (`--grip-cast`, cap-scale contact + a short drop):** the lab port made `--control-chrome` the lit button chrome, swollen under a 16-28px cap and drawing its inset under-shade as a line inside a white circle. **The switch's thumb inherited the exception with the role 2026-08-08** — same grip, same reading, same disabled stand-down. **The slider thumb's hairline is GONE (2026-08-17, the fill-first lens reaching the grips):** the switch thumb never had one — a hairline the colour of whatever sits behind a MOVING part is a halo in one of its two homes — and the solved `--control-edge` there was the border-first identity's residue; the cast is what lifts a grip.

That inheritance was asserted at REST and diverged under state until 2026-08-08: the slider thumb, being a `.kui-mark`, took the family's `--tone-soft` disabled arm while the switch's kept `--color-thumb`, so in dark the two grips the sentence above calls one role landed at opposite ends of the lightness scale and the slider's sat DARKER than its own rail — the invisible handle `--color-thumb` was minted to prevent, returning under `disabled`. The slider now takes the switch's answer — **and both were re-tuned 2026-08-17 (Kushagra: "the thumb should be lighter also in disabled"): a dead grip DIMS to `--disabled-dim` (70%) of `--color-thumb`, in lockstep across both grips** — still the most findable object on a rail whose track sits at a few percent alpha, while clearly standing down; the full melt to `--tone-soft` remains the recorded failure (one indistinguishable capsule), and the full-strength fill read live beside a dimmed track. The argument is the switch's own, and it was always about grips rather than about switches — a grip's POSITION is the value, so greying it erases the reading instead of dimming it, the same sentence that stops the family's disabled rule erasing a checked checkbox's glyph. What still stands down for both is the cast: "always" is about the world, never about state. No catch: a gradient on a near-white grip is invisible. **Its fill left the seal the same hour ("dark shouldn't be dark"):** `--color-thumb`, the value-control family's third role — light keeps the seal, dark goes near-white (`--neutral-12`, iOS's own posture), because a grip must be the most findable object on the rail and a seal-dark handle on a dark rail was nearly invisible.

  **One lighting model, the palette its source of truth, and the edge is not special (finesse passes, 2026-08-04, Kushagra).** Three constraints closed it in sequence: the edge should read lighter at top and darker at bottom; whatever consumes shadow must share one lighting model, including shadow itself; and contrast must reach the edge. Resolution — *add depth, change nothing else*: `--surface-chrome` **is `var(--shadow-4)`, and `--control-chrome` is the lab's LIT RUNG authored whole, not a palette composition at all** (both corrected here 2026-08-20; this sentence had said row 3 and "the crisp inset rim plus `var(--shadow-2)`" since 2026-08-07, and the lab port moved both on 2026-08-17). The surface role takes row 4 because that is the lab's SOLID card — contact plus one 24/64 drop — while row 5's two stacked drops belong to what GLASS transmits, which still derives from row 5 through `glassTransmitRows`; dark's inset rim-light went with the same judgment, since the lab's dark solid has none and it doubled against the solid lighting's top catch. The control role is contact + one 8/20 drop with light folding a bottom shade into the pigment and dark carrying none, and the white inset rim is retired — the gradient catch (`--control-light`) says what the 1px machined line was there to say, and the palette's row 2 blast read swollen on a 32px box. Row 2 survives as the glass transmit source, the same division drawn one family up between rows 4 and 5. **The one-source-of-shadow-truth claim narrowed rather than broke**: tuning a palette row still tunes every consumer of that reach, and what left the palette is the two authored rungs the lab judged directly, which is stated in config beside them. The border stays the same `--tone-border` every surface wears, which keeps the edge sharp and puts it inside the contrast system for free; the top edge reads lighter because every palette shadow falls downward. Two dead ends recorded so they stay dead: an inset ring beside a transparent border (paints inside the border strip — two lines), and a raw-alpha border (soft, and blind to `contrast="high"`). Laws: every `box-shadow` declaration in the shipped stylesheets reads a chrome role and no rule names `--shadow-N` directly — the COUNT is pinned once, in the consumer paragraph above, and is deliberately not restated here (this sentence said *two* while the law asserted six, audit 2026-08-09: the third time this number drifted between two homes); the chromes compose their stated rows, and the two roles must name DIFFERENT rows, since asserting only the surface's would pass with every role pointed at one; mounted, an elevated card's computed shadow equals **row 4** exactly (the floating role's is row 3 — §22), the control chrome is read as the LINES the generator emitted in each appearance rather than as a row reference, and the card's border is identical to the flat world's.
- **Escapes and blocks** through `style={{ boxShadow: "var(--shadow-N)" }}` — lawless but visible, as `style` has always been.

**Box's `shadow` prop shipped for a day and died as a taxonomy leak (Kushagra):** Box is layout, shadow is paint, and a typed prop added nothing but a blessing the taxonomy forbids. A `@ts-expect-error` law pins the refusal, alongside Card's.

```
tone (hue) + emphasis (rung) -> role-token bundle -> scale steps -> generated OKLCH values
```

Same pattern as everywhere: expose intent, resolve to values.

### Axes are derived, not assigned

Questions about a component's nature determine its axis set:
- carries meaning -> **tone**
- an action with loudness -> **emphasis**
- can sit on a busy background -> **bordered**
- ~~has height above the page -> **elevation**~~ (deleted 2026-08-03: nothing ever varied it per call site — it was a component fact, and this list should have caught it)
- **can float over content** -> **material**
- interactive -> **states**
- has a size step -> **size**

Answer those and any component's axes fall out, including ones not yet built. **Controls** use tone x emphasis x bordered x states x size. **Surfaces** add material. **Interactive surfaces** reuse the control state machine from a subtler base.

**Material is derived from floating, not from being a container** (corrected 2026-08-01). The earlier reading gave material to surfaces only, which was wrong in both directions: a Card sitting in a solid layout has nothing to defend against, and a Button floating over a photo does. Material follows the question "can something busy be behind this," and that question is asked of buttons too.

### What this finally fixes

The system now *knows* what is primary (`emphasis="loud"`), so hierarchy is legible to tooling — a lint rule can flag two loud actions in one scope — which a pure appearance API structurally cannot, because it never learns the button's job. **Hierarchy is convention plus optional lint, not mechanism:** no auto-scoping, no auto-demotion of a second loud button. Silent demotion is the kind of magic that is impossible to debug when it fires wrongly.

### The cost, named honestly

Two axes is a grid, and most cells resolve cleanly because tone swaps hue while emphasis swaps rung. One cell needs a real answer: **`loud + neutral`, the loud grey button.** It is a legitimate and common pattern (a near-black primary), but it breaks on the generated ladder rather than on the axis model — `loud` fills with step 9, and neutral step 9 sits near L .62, a mid-grey that reads secondary or disabled instead of primary. A convincing black button wants roughly L .2. This is the same failure as the bright-hue case in section 7 (a fixed ladder producing a wrong-looking solid for one hue, here for the absence of one), so it is resolved with the solid band, not here.

Name the loudness axis **emphasis**, never "primary/secondary," which smuggles the conflation back in, and never `variant`, which invites the docs to spend forever explaining that the prop is not about looks.

---

## 10. Surfaces

**Decision (amended 2026-08-03, re-amended 2026-08-04): a surface is tone x emphasis x bordered x material. There is no elevation axis, and no call site ever chooses a shadow — the one sanctioned shadow is Theme `depth="elevated"`, an app identity chosen once (§5, and "The shadow palette" below).**

### Elevation: deleted (2026-08-03, Kushagra)

The section that stood here defined a four-level shadow ladder (flat / raised / floating / overlay). It died on first contact with the eye — "shadows for elevation is a no go" — and the post-mortem showed the axis was over-modelled from the start: **nothing ever chose elevation at a call site.** §11's own table fixed it per component (a Card is never floating, a Menu is never flat, a Dialog is always an overlay), and §9's rule — axes are derived, not assigned — should have caught that a prop nobody varies is a component fact, not an axis.
