**The segmented control is a RADIO GROUP, and that is the load-bearing decision.** Base UI offers two primitives that could carry the box: `ToggleGroup`, which announces `role="group"` holding `aria-pressed` buttons, and `RadioGroup`, which announces `role="radiogroup"`. Picking one of several IS a radio group, arrow keys then select as they move (every platform's segmented behaviour), and we already wrap RadioGroup for Radio. The multi-select formatting case — bold / italic / underline — is genuinely a set of toggle buttons and genuinely a different component: `Toggle` in a `ToggleGroup`, shipped 2026-08-31 (§34), whose group is always multiple for the same reason this one is never. This also closes the question the 2026-08-06 audit deferred: how selection state is spelled on a control the system did not have. It is spelled as a real role, from the primitive, not as an aria attribute bolted onto a Button.

**The geometry is §4's hosted-control rule with N hosts.** The TRACK rides the height ladder, so a segmented control stands level with the Button beside it in a toolbar — law-asserted against a mounted Button's RENDERED box. That parenthetical used to say "not against a token" while the law compared two declared `min-height`s, which is a token identity that cannot fail, and it was green over a track rendering 2px taller than its Button in all 24 cells (audit 2026-08-19, D2): the skeleton's transparent border still occupies layout under `border-box`. The track now stands the skeleton's border down — by width, never by naming the painted variable — so its box IS the ladder rung, which is also what keeps its corner honest at `full`. Each segment is the channel minus a designed inset on every side — the switch's own sentence, thumb = track − 2 × inset, with N thumbs — so the inset is designed and the box is what derives. The segments read `--kui-ct-h` by inheritance from the track and stamp no index of their own, which is what makes it impossible for the two boxes to disagree about what size they are. The corner is concentric (§6): inner = outer − inset, spelled as "never subtract more corner than there is" rather than as a clamp at zero, because the tokens-only law allows no zero literal and this system has already been bitten by a corner that asked CSS to work something out (§6's 9999px capsule).

**`segmentInset` is numerically `switchInset` and is deliberately not shared yet.** Both mean a grip sitting in a channel. The rule this repo follows is that the second member of a family self-keys and the THIRD promotes (the field family on TextArea, the mark family on the slider thumb); a shared inset would want a family name, and renaming a shipped token with one consumer to earn it is a change without a third caller to justify it. When a third channel-and-grip control arrives, the promotion is the rename.

**The track is a WELL, so the whole control leaves the look axis** — the switch's argument verbatim (§19): a channel is felt for, not read as a small surface, so there is no boundary-versus-fill trade for the axis to make. **Its VALUE left `--color-track` 2026-08-24** (Kushagra: "I see no reason for it to be any other color than text fields or areas"): the channel wears `--dress-field-fill`, one gray for field, area and this track, while the instruments keep the deeper `--color-track` (slider rail, switch off-track, progress well). What still separates this track from a field is the EDGE, not the value — a field wears the dress hairline and a well has none — and the law is the AGREEMENT with a mounted TextField in both appearances, not the token name, so it fails the day either side moves alone. The chosen segment is the GRIP: `--color-thumb`, one value across rest, hover and press (a grip does not fill, it moves), and it **casts always**, flat world included — but not on glass (2026-08-24, Kushagra: "When glass, segmented control's thumb should render as neutral gray like hover in menu, and the entire control gets glass"): **when the track IS the pane the thumb is `--material-row-wash`, flat, with the chosen label back at the full ink** (`--color-thumb-label` was minted against the near-white fill and has nothing to sit on once the fill is a wash; the rule lives in surfaces.css because a component sheet may not name a material and recipes.css's material region may not touch a colour), and **inside someone else's pane the grip keeps its pigment and loses only its lift** — the in-pane and is-pane cases are told apart by the fill, and both are law-mounted in both appearances. **Its label is `--color-thumb-label`, minted 2026-08-19 and stated beside the fill it answers to.** `thumbFill` was designed for grips carrying no text and goes near-white in both modes; the segmented control is its first consumer with a label on it, and in dark `--color-thumb` and `--color-text` are the same token by construction — measured 1.00:1, `contrast="high"` moving neither, a blank pill where the current value should be. Both entries of the new pair are values the generator had already solved (the seal's ink in light, `--neutral-contrast` in dark, which is the APCA-chosen pairing for exactly this fill), so nothing new was judged. **A disabled control dims the channel and both labels too** — all three of its resting colours are non-tone roles the shared remap cannot reach, which is the slider rail's own 2026-08-07 defect three components on. That is the **seventh box-shadow consumer** and the grips' exception a third time rather than a new one — recorded in the count law, which is where such a decision is supposed to fail first.

**There is no indicator element in the segmented control, and the absence is the design.** Base UI's Tabs ships one because it measures the active tab for you; RadioGroup ships none, so a gliding thumb here means writing the measurement ourselves — a mechanism whose only consumer is a motion that has not been designed (§8's pass owns it), which is the entropy this repo keeps paying for (the curtain, deleted 2026-08-17). The selected segment paints its own box: no JS at interaction time, and the honest thing to draw when nothing travels.

**Tabs, by contrast, ships the indicator on day one, because Base UI hands it over free.** `Tabs.Indicator` measures the active tab and publishes `--active-tab-left/right/top/bottom/width/height`, with a pre-hydration script so the rule is correct on first paint rather than flashing at the origin. It is rendered by `TabsList` itself and deliberately **not exported**: the rule is structure, not API, and a consumer who has to remember to place it is one who will forget. **It is drawn by `left` + `width`** — Base UI's own spelling, and everyone else's. It shipped drawn by both inline edges instead, on the argument that two edges can travel at two speeds and it cost the same two declarations either way; the second half was false (audit 2026-08-19, D4). Base UI computes `--active-tab-right` in the list's SCROLL coordinate space while CSS resolves `right` against the containing block's PADDING box, and the two agree only while the bar fits — measured, an overflowing bar rendered the rule at ZERO width, degrading a pixel per pixel of overflow on the way there. Two edges is a thing to reach for when a motion needs them, and it will need a coordinate fix first. **Physical `left`, not the logical inset this package otherwise prefers**, because Base UI's measurement is physical and the two halves have to agree or an RTL bar puts the rule on the wrong side.

**That indicator re-measures on every selection change, which is JS at interaction time.** Named rather than waved through: it is the third bounded exception, beside the floating layer's flight measurement and the lens (§10, §22) — built on mount, resize and change, never on hover, press or scroll. CSS cannot read a sibling's box, so an indicator that is one object rather than N painted boxes has no other implementation.

**The active tab is marked by INK and the rule, never by a louder fill or a heavier label.** A fill makes the active tab read as a button among links; a heavier weight reflows the whole bar on every switch, because a semibold label is wider than a medium one. The ink pair is the TONE-LESS foreground roles (`--color-text` / `--color-text-muted`), not the ink trio, because tabs refuse tone and the trio only exists under a stamped `[data-tone]` — which also means a bar dropped onto a tone-forward surface follows that surface, exactly as Text does (§11, §15). The bar's hairline is `--color-border`, the quiet no-family edge, law-asserted to resolve the same colour a Separator does: the line under a tab bar and the line between two things are one object (§7).

**The part vocabulary diverges from shadcn/ui, and it is the first time.** Menu, Select, Dialog and AlertDialog all took shadcn's names with credit; shadcn's tabs are `Tabs / TabsList / TabsTrigger / TabsContent`. `TabsTrigger` cannot come across: in this package a *trigger* opens a floating layer — MenuTrigger, SelectTrigger, DialogTrigger, AlertDialogTrigger — and a tab opens nothing. `TabsTab` and `TabsPanel` follow the roles they announce. The shape of the composition is still shadcn's.

**Both components stamp `data-tone="neutral"`, and the stamp is load-bearing** (audit 2026-08-19, D3/D5). `--tone-*` exists only inside a `[data-tone]` block, so a control that stamps none has no hover and no press at all — the quiet rung's sources are invalid at computed-value time and fall to transparent, which is what a tab shipped as. And the roles INHERIT, so an unstamped bar takes whatever family it is dropped inside: measured, a tab bar inside a `destructive` section hovered red. The segmented control shipped with the opposite error, `accent`, justified as the chosen segment needing a family to resolve against — it does not read one — so what the stamp actually reached was every unchosen segment's hover, blue in light and an inverted near-opaque navy in dark. Neutral, stamped, on both.

**The segments sit flush, gap 0** (Kushagra, 2026-08-19). The skeleton's `gap` is the icon-to-label distance and it was reaching the track by inheritance — up to 16px of bare well between segments at coarse size 4, re-priced by pointer and size while deaf to density, owned by no config entry. Overridden as `gap`, never as `--kui-ct-gap`: a segment is itself a `.kui-control` and reads that token for its own innards.

**`size` lives on `TabsList`, once** — Menu's own reversal (2026-08-09): a bar of tabs at mixed sizes is not a thing anyone means. The join keys `[data-size]` per element, so the tabs still carry the attribute; a React context is what stops them from being given different ones.

**Glass: the segmented control's track is a pane and a tab bar is not.** The track expresses the theme's material where a backdrop exists and resolves solid everywhere else (§10's selectivity), and it scopes its subtree, so anything inside a glass track resolves `on-glass` — one glass per stack, structurally, with nobody typing anything. Tabs paint no fill and no box, so there is nothing behind them to defocus and `material` never reaches them.

**THE TRAVELLING HIGHLIGHT (2026-08-23, Kushagra, judged in the "Clip vs Physics" bench; §8).** Both ship it now, and the prediction above held in one direction and not the other. Tabs' indicator was already an element of its own, so it needed nothing structural; the segmented control shipped with NO indicator on the recorded argument that a gliding thumb would mean writing a measurement for a motion nobody had designed — and its own comment named the door it was leaving open: *"when the motion pass wants one object gliding between homes, the measuring hook arrives with it."* It has arrived.

**The recipe is the switch's edge trick applied BETWEEN homes.** One object drawn by its two inline edges, and the edge FACING the destination takes the shorter clock — 320ms against 480 — so the highlight stretches toward the tab you picked and gathers itself as the far edge catches up. Both edges ride `calm`, the ~6.8% one-crossing spring: the character is one spring and the asymmetry is entirely in the two durations, which is the only shape that keeps damping sacred (§8). Two edges on one clock is a photograph being slid, which is the motion this replaced. `controlMotion.travel` (420, the switch thumb's channel crossing) is deliberately not reused — a highlight moving a whole segment's width answers a different question, and the new pair straddles the benchmark rather than replacing it.

**THE CHANNEL HAS WALLS (2026-08-25, Kushagra, from the glass preview against the bench).** The calm spring overshoots ~6.8% of travel, and on a flight into an END seat that overshoot has nowhere legal to go: measured, a full first-to-last jump on a 360px track put the grip's edge **14.11px outside the track** at t=170ms — identically in solid and on glass; only the visibility differed (a white grip over a white page against a grip poking out of a ringed pane over a photo backdrop), which is why it was reported as a glass defect. The bench's own lean rule already states the principle — *"a lean can never cross a boundary: it always points inward"* — and the flight now obeys the same wall. **The wall is GEOMETRY, not a second spring**: the two clocks and the calm character are untouched; the spring rides the two REGISTERED `<length>` custom properties the measurement already writes (an unregistered custom property transitions discretely, so the registration is what makes a spring expressible on one at all), and the painted inset is that value floored at the channel inset — `max()` at the point of use. An interior flight never reaches the floor, so every interior flight is byte-identical; a flight into an end seat has its leading edge arrive at the wall and hold while the trailing edge is still flying on its own clock, so the overshoot is spent as a **squash against the wall** instead of an escape through it — what a grip with momentum hitting the end of a real channel does, and iOS's own behaviour at the track ends. Rejected: `overflow: clip` on the track (pins the visible edge at the BOX rather than the channel — the grip would intrude into the inset frame — shaves the grip's cast, and is the bench's rejected side by name), and a non-overshooting curve on wall-bound flights (the component would have to know its destination is an end, and "one character, the asymmetry entirely in the durations" dies with a per-destination spring). The registration retires the old var() fallbacks ("an unmeasured thumb lands on the first seat") by making them unreachable — the `[hidden]`-until-measured gate is the one guard, stated as such. The wall law sweeps a seized flight and carries a calibration half: the RAW inset must still cross the wall mid-flight, so a build that quietly tamed the spring fails as loudly as one that let the grip escape. A track forced below its own min-content puts SEATS outside the channel (the flex line overflows and the skeleton centres it); the grip does not follow them out — that geometry is the caller's break, and the squeezed-fixture law was re-cut to bind the floors without forcing it.

**Direction is the one fact CSS cannot see** — a stylesheet knows the value a property animates TO and never the value it left — so it is stamped. Tabs gets it FREE: Base UI already publishes `data-activation-direction` on its indicator, including the `none` that is exactly the first paint, so the whole of Tabs' motion is CSS and the component gained no JavaScript at all. The segmented control has to write its own, which is the asymmetry that also settles the second edge below.

**The tab rule is drawn by two edges again, with the second one DERIVED.** The 2026-08-19 revert was right about the defect and the file said what a return would need first: `--active-tab-right` is `scrollWidth − left − width` in the list's SCROLL space while CSS resolves `right` against the containing block's PADDING box, so an overflowing bar drew a zero-width rule. `calc(100% - left - width)` derives the same edge from the pair Base UI computes in ONE space, against the `100%` that IS the containing block. Measured on a bar overflowing by 61px: the rule spans 91.69 against a 91.67 tab, where the old spelling drew 0. **The bar gained its WALLS 2026-08-25** (with the segmented channel's, same day, above): the spring rides the registered `--kui-tab-left/right` pair and the painted insets floor through `max()` — the first tab rests at the bar's very start, so every flight back to it used to carry the rule ~14px out of the box. **The floor is ADAPTIVE, `min(target, 0%)` per edge, and that is the half the segmented control did not need**: a static floor at the bar's edge re-commits the coordinate trap above as a clamp, because an overflow-region tab's resting `right` inset is legitimately negative — so the wall is the bar's edge while the destination sits inside the box and the destination's own seat when it sits past it, where a flight arrives with no overshoot at all. The derivation gets one home (`--kui-tab-*-target`, the instant copy) consumed by both the sprung pair and the wall, because one name cannot be both the spring and the target. A flight to the last tab of a bar the tabs do not fill deliberately keeps its overshoot past the last *label*: the rule is ink on a rail and the rail continues — the wall is the box, not the label. Tabs still gains no JavaScript.

**The panel seam is the FIFTH bounded exception, and it was found by a performance pass rather than declared by its author (2026-08-31).** A submenu's child panel aligns its first row with the row that opened it, and that distance is the parent panel's own padding plus its border — two density-derived lengths that live in CSS and that JavaScript can only ask for. `panelSeam` (menu.tsx) is handed to Base UI's positioner as a FUNCTION, so it is evaluated three times per position pass and re-run whenever floating-ui re-solves, which includes scroll inside the parent panel — the clause each of the other four exceptions buys its licence by refusing. It carried no exemption and no mention anywhere, and the enforcing law reported the file clean, because that law could only see the machinery AROUND a measurement (an observer, a frame, a listener) and never a measurement itself. **The law was widened rather than the mechanism removed**: `getComputedStyle` and `getBoundingClientRect` are now banned shapes with a named exemption each, so the doctrine's exceptions are enumerated in the law and not only in this file. And the mechanism stays because it was MEASURED and is not a cost — a scroll frame with the menu open is 8.33ms against 8.33ms closed, 8.50ms at 6x CPU throttle. Caching it was refused: the function returns 0 while the parent panel is not yet in the DOM, and a cache that kept that 0 re-commits the exact defect the seam's own law exists to catch.

**The segmented thumb costs a measurement, and it is the FOURTH bounded exception to "no JS at interaction time"** — beside the flight's measurement, the lens and Tabs' own re-measure. Bounded the same way: it runs on a selection change and on a resize, never on hover, press, focus or scroll, and it writes two lengths rather than driving a frame loop. **Arithmetic over an index was the alternative and it is wrong, measured**: `flex: 1 1 0` gives every segment an equal share while the track sizes itself (425.3 / 425.3 / 425.3 for three labels of three lengths), but constrain the track and `min-width: auto` binds on the longest — 62.0 / 62.0 / 72.0 in a 200px box, where the arithmetic answers 65.3. A squeezed track is a toolbar on a narrow window. The selection is watched through `data-checked` rather than through a re-render, because an uncontrolled `RadioGroup` holds its value inside Base UI and never re-renders this component: the first spelling fired its effect once in a component's lifetime and the thumb held 56.9px while another segment was genuinely checked.

**The chosen segment gave up its fill.** Two boxes painting one grip would mean the old segment's fill blinking out as the thumb glided away from it, so the fill and the cast moved to the thumb and the segment keeps only its ink — which switches INSTANTLY while the box travels, §8's split rather than an oversight.

**The keyboard is not exempted, and that is a deliberate departure from the bench.** The bench jumps on arrow keys, on the rule that keys are not travel; its own reason is that gliding across intermediate stops would be false motion, and arrow keys here move exactly one position, so nothing is crossed that was not passed through. Exempting them would cost a modality listener at interaction time to buy a distinction the argument does not make at this step size. Reopen it if a keyboard jump of more than one ever exists.

**Both were audited the day after they shipped (2026-08-19, ultracode, 31 agents, every finding adversarially verified and the three worst re-measured by hand), and the findings are fixed.** Twenty-three survived refutation, deduping to nine defects and eight laws that could not fail. **The pattern was one mistake made five times: the components inherited the shared control layer by ASSERTION rather than by measurement, and then the laws were written from the same assertion** — so each defect came with a confident comment stating the false premise and a law reading the comment's own inputs instead of the rendered output. Two of the five are defects this repo had already closed and cited BY NAME in the comment that re-committed them (the border term is audit D3; the non-tone disabled role is the slider rail). Also closed: `nativeButton` inference on a tab rendered as a link, which is the `type="button"`-on-an-anchor trap the package has now closed five times, and `nativeButton` reopened on a segment, which breaks Space selection on the same Base UI primitive the mark family closed it on. The laws went 42 → 51, every one falsified, and the file gained the `@ts-expect-error` refusal probes it shipped without — which is how the reopened prop got in.

Left open and recorded: **RTL arrow keys move focus, and on the segmented control the value, in the wrong direction.** Base UI's composite navigation reads its own React direction context, not the DOM's `dir`, and `DirectionProvider` is rendered in four files here — none of them these. It is pre-existing and systemic (Slider has had it since 2026-08-06), the fix is package-wide (promote the floating layer's ambient direction, or render the provider in `Theme`), and the segmented control is the worst instance because its arrows change the value as they move.

+229 bytes gzipped for both at first ship. The two designed numbers — `segmentInset` and `tabRule` — are taste, and so is the 20px segment at fine/compact/size 1 (four pixels under its size token; measured NOT a WCAG 2.5.8 failure, since that SC measures circle intersection and the centres sit 47px apart, and larger than a size-1 Button hosted in a size-1 field, which ships at 18).
## 27. Shell (spec'd 2026-08-16 from v1's corpse; SHIPPED the same day)

The app frame: the regions an application tool is made of, and the one component family that is *supposed* to key off the viewport — §13's carve-out ("only Shell and page-gutter concerns key off the viewport") finally gets its named consumer, and §18's 75rem boundary gets the rendered shell it has been waiting to be re-judged against. The spec was written against v1's shipped Shell (~4,300 lines TS, 643 CSS, 46 test files), and most of it is deletions; LOG 2026-08-16 carries the autopsy. v1's own audit report ships with open P0s (a static property shared across every Panel instance, uncleaned throttle closures, missing resize-handle labels) — the bugs are downstream of the architecture, which is why this is a re-derivation and not a port.

**Anatomy: seven parts, flat exports in the family's vocabulary** — `Shell`, `ShellHeader`, `ShellRail`, `ShellSidebar`, `ShellContent`, `ShellInspector`, `ShellBottom`, plus `ShellTrigger`. Content is required and renders `<main>`; Header renders `<header>`; the landmark structure is by construction, v1's one unambiguously right call kept whole. The root is a Theme descendant like everything else and must never be the element portals land in (§20's body constraint applies to it by inheritance).

**Rail and Sidebar are two independent columns, and the renaming is the design.** v1 shipped a three-state Sidebar (`collapsed | thin | expanded`) AND a Rail+Panel pair, then needed a runtime exclusivity rule, `displayName`-based child scanning, and a close-cascade with callback meta explaining who closed whom — because `thin` was a rail wearing a sidebar's name, so the same region existed twice under two names. Here: the **rail** is the narrow icon column that switches sections; the **sidebar** is the wide column. One column of navigation = Sidebar. Icons only = Rail. VS Code/Slack = both. Nothing excludes anything because nothing overlaps; the left edge holds at most two columns because there are at most two slots. Deleted with the collision: `thin`/`toggleModes`, the exclusivity warning, the composition-detection scanning, and the rail→panel cascade (not universal — VS Code's are independent, Slack's rail is permanent — so it is an app opinion, three lines at the call site, never a shell rule with a conflict-resolution protocol).

**The header is full-width by definition (Kushagra, verbatim: "if it isn't wide it's not shell header, then it's content header").** A criterion, not a layout option: the shell's geometry is fixed — header row across the top, columns beneath — and the Linear posture (sidebar full-height, header only over the content) is expressed by composition: a shell with NO ShellHeader and a header inside ShellContent. The shell never chooses between geometries because there is only one.

**One element per pane, always mounted; `presentation` is dress, not a component.** v1's Sidebar early-returns a different tree per breakpoint (`if (isOverlay) return <Sheet.Root>…`), so crossing a window boundary unmounts the nav — scroll, focus, state gone on a resize — while `className`, `style`, `resizable`, persistence and peek silently die on the overlay branch and the accessible name hardcodes to "Navigation". That is the "mechanism with two implementations owes an agreement law" rule (LOG 2026-08-06) in the form where the law is unwritable: they cannot agree, they are different components. Here a pane is ONE element wearing `data-presentation`, and `fixed | overlay` are two CSS treatments of the same box (v1's own stacked mode proves the technique — absolute position + transform, pure CSS). What overlay genuinely owes beyond paint — scrim, Escape, focus containment — is behavior attached to the same element post-mount, the dialog family's machinery pointed at an in-flow box; it engages only when the overlay treatment is live, and first paint never needs it because containment cannot matter before interaction. `stacked` is deferred until a real screen asks (the tone-set widening rule).

**State lives on the pane, in the library's one controlled-state pattern.** v1 declared intent on panes but stored state on the Root — the straddle that forced child scanning, a `breakpointReady` gate, init-time `onExpand` misfires (its own audit's fixed P0), and a `setTimeout(0)` controlled-sync. Here each pane owns `open` exactly as Dialog does: uncontrolled by default, `open`/`onOpenChange` to control, nothing else. No second controlled channel (v1 ran `open` + `size` + `expandedSize` per pane, three spellings of two facts with per-pane meta vocabularies its own audit flagged as inconsistent). The shell never needs a pane's state before the pane renders: panes are grid/flex children that state their own inline size, content takes the remainder — no arithmetic in the Root, so no registry of sizes, no initialization order, nothing to sync. What crosses the shell is exactly one thing: `ShellTrigger target="sidebar"` must find its pane — each pane registers its toggle by name on mount, the trigger looks it up, stamps `aria-expanded`/`aria-controls`, and composes via `render`. That registry is single-purpose and carries no layout state.

**The responsive default is CSS-resolved, "auto until touched."** The server cannot know the window (§18: `useWindowClass` returns `null` honestly), and v1's JS-measured breakpoints are the root of its whole init bug class. A pane that nobody has touched renders `data-state="auto"`, and the stylesheet resolves what auto means per window class through §18's boundary queries — so first paint is right with zero script, on both server and client, and hydration cannot mismatch because both render the same attribute. The user's first toggle stamps an explicit state that wins over any boundary. **A hide out-ranks a layout rule, and that is stated rather than left to source order (2026-08-20).** Every hide selector carries the `.kui-surface` class its pane already wears, because the two rules that decide how a DISPLAYED pane lays itself out — the pane's own `:has(> .kui-shell-scroll)` column rule and surfaces.css's shared `:has(> .kui-scroll-area:only-child)` — sit at the same specificity and both land later in the cascade. Without the class the whole mechanism was dead on the anatomy the component recommends: a closed pane holding a ScrollArea computed `display: flex` at every width, and a nav column whose scroller was its only child rested OPEN on a narrow window. Found by porting the builder onto the frame, with all 46 laws green — every one of them mounts a pane holding a text node, which is the one composition where the hide has no opponent (the degenerate-fixture rule, LOG 2026-08-20). Consequences that fall out rather than being engineered: no open/close callbacks can fire at mount (nothing in JS decided anything), and the window-class boundary crossing moves panes without React involvement. The hook remains for apps that need to *know* (§18's post-mount contract). This makes shell.css the first stylesheet keyed on the §18 boundaries — sanctioned: the §18 law ("no token answers the window class") already scopes itself to the GENERATED sheet by its own wording, so shipping needed no restatement — shell.css is a handwritten component sheet inside §13's named viewport carve-out, and its own node law pins the boundary literal to config's `narrowMedia`, verbatim and exactly once.

**Posture is ONE BOOLEAN per pane, and what it becomes is DERIVED (rewritten 2026-08-20; LOG carries the full record).** The shipped axis was `panes="flush" | "floating"` on the root, and it named the wrong thing: it kept every pane in its own grid column and put gaps between them, so nothing overlapped and a creation app's canvas could not run behind its own layers panel. v1 had the missing half under a third name — `presentation="stacked"`, documented verbatim as *"positions above content without displacing it"* — and never joined it to `inset`, which carried the detached dress; v1's Toolbar then spelled `floating` as detached-with-a-margin and `inset` as bleeding edge-to-edge, the exact opposite of the Shell's, one library, two words, swapped (that mode is `m="bleed"` since 2026-08-20, so the collision retires rather than being carried forward). Floating is those halves welded: leave the tiling, sit over the content, wear the detached dress while doing it.

**The prop is `flush`, defaulting true, and nothing states the outcome.** `flush={false}` says only "pull this pane off the frame". **A pane floats if the content is underneath it, and the content is underneath it only if the content is itself flush; otherwise it grounds** — its own surface on the app's ground, the card relationship at pane scale. One boolean per pane reaches all four postures: everything flush is the tiled app frame (VS Code); non-flush nav over flush content is the canvas app (Figma, Womp); flush chrome around non-flush content is the console (Canva, Xcode); everything non-flush is the all-cards frame the shipped axis actually produced. The three-value spelling was written first and REFUSED, and its refusal is why the naming problem evaporated: it let a call site state a floating sidebar beside a grounded content card and get a panel hovering over a card for no reason, a lie the derivation cannot be told. `grounded` and `floating` are outcomes, not vocabulary. The root prop is DELETED (Kushagra: *"Why do I need `panes='flush'` to make content raised?"*) on the standing one-fact-one-home argument — a root default plus per-pane override needs a precedence rule somebody has to remember. **Refused with its cost stated:** above the content but hard against the window edge; you cannot be over the content without also being pulled in from the edges, which v1 could express only because its two switches happened to combine.

**Two things arrive free, and both are evidence earlier calls were right.** GLASS: §10's selectivity says a surface expresses the theme's material only where a backdrop is stated, so a pane in its own column has nothing to bend and a pane over the canvas has the canvas. **The pane STATES it, and the posture no longer infers it (2026-08-29, Kushagra: "all panels should support backdrop prop, we already have precedence for it"; "I dont think content should ever get glass, panels are fine").** The five panels take `backdrop` on Card's line verbatim — the prop when stated, the ambient `<Box backdrop>` region when not — which is what keeps full-window vibrancy reachable (a macOS sidebar is flush against the window edge AND translucent over the wallpaper). What it replaces was an inference from `flush`: a non-flush pane stated `backdrop: true` on the argument that "something is behind it either way — the content if it floats, the ground if it does not". The first half is true; **the second half was never true of a MATERIAL** — a grounded pane sits on the app's ground, which is a flat colour, and selectivity is precisely the rule that glass is expressed only where something PASSES behind. Measured under `material="regular"` before the change: a `flush={false}` content pane resolved a 49% white veil, `blur(4px) saturate(2.07)` and its own lens map — the largest box in the app bending a flat fill, re-minted on every resize. **This CLOSES the open item recorded one paragraph down** rather than narrowing it: JS no longer has to guess what CSS will derive, because nothing is derived. The inference cannot return as a default either — a prop whose default is computed from a different prop is the shape this system refuses — so `flush` is now a statement about the FRAME and nothing else, and the two questions are independent at every posture. **`ShellContent` takes no `backdrop` at all, and that is the family's one asymmetry**: shell.css derives floating as *a pane floats if the content is underneath it*, so the content is by construction the one pane nothing is ever underneath — the bottom of the stack. It passes a hard `false`, contradicting even an ambient region marked around the whole frame, because such a region is a claim about what is behind the FRAME and the work area is not in front of it. The escape is the system's own sentence rather than a prop: **a solid surface HOSTS glass** (§10, 2026-08-19), so a vibrant region inside the work area is a `<Box backdrop>` or a `<Card backdrop>` placed there — law-asserted, not claimed. Refused in the TYPE, which is the half no mount can assert. DEPTH: `[data-depth="flat"]` re-declares `--surface-edge: initial` in surfaces.css, so a non-flush pane in a flat world draws its boundary exactly as a Card does, with no shell rule at all.

**Mechanism, and the one thing it does not buy.** Flush is keyed on the pane's own attribute (`[data-flush]`), so the seam rules are unchanged in substance. Floating is `z-index: 1` on a grid item with NO `position` — grid items are z-index-aware while static, which is what leaves the overlay treatment's `position: absolute; z-index: 2` uncontested in either source order, and what keeps a floating rail and a floating sidebar tiling correctly beside each other (out of flow their grid areas both collapse to zero width and they would land stacked on the same edge). The content reaches under a floating pane by growing its own grid area to that pane's start or end line — `grid-template-areas` mints those names for free — so it grows only on the sides something floats on, and a flush header above a floating sidebar still pushes the content down. **SPACING is provably not a per-pane constant**: requiring flush|flush to be 0 and non-flush|non-flush to be `g` fixes non-flush|flush at `g/2` for every split of the gap between the frame's padding, the grid's gap and the panes' margins — two non-flush panes each pay a share and a flush pane pays none. A non-flush pane therefore pays in FULL on every side, which is exact against the frame edge, and the all-cards frame keeps the half-and-half construction because it is exact there and only there. **Against a flush neighbour "pays in full" was WRONG, and the sidebar's scrollbar is what exposed it (2026-08-30, Kushagra: *"there's gap between sidebar and shell content, which isn't needed when only one of them is flush"*).** The claim rested on "a flush pane pays nothing", which is true of margins and false of what the eye sees: a flush pane PADS (the 2026-08-21 safe area), paints no fill, and draws no hairline toward a card (the 2026-08-29 seams), so its padding is indistinguishable from the gap — measured, sidebar rows at x=272 and the content card at x=296, a 24px void with the overlay scrollbar pinned to the pane's invisible wall in the middle of it. **A grounded content now pays no margin toward a flush pane; the flush pane's padding IS the boundary's one share of air.** The card's share goes rather than the pane's padding, because the padding is a safe area serving the pane's own rows — and the row press expander reaches `-1 * --kui-sf-p` symmetrically, so a per-side padding would push a row's highlight out the opposite wall. The subject is the CONTENT pane only (in the grounded regime the content is THE card); the card's edge lands on the flush pane's wall, and the sidebar's scrollbar sits 2px from the card's hairline — the divider's own scrollbar, macOS's arrangement. The guards are the reach rules' (closed, overlay and an interposed non-flush sidebar all buy no discount), and the narrow media block restates the restore for `presentation="auto"`, which resolves to overlay there. **Deferred, logged, not built** (Kushagra: *"lets not worry about it"*): two non-flush panes adjacent double to 2g, which closing means about eight `:has()` rules of the form "if the thing next to you is non-flush, give it air" — no app in front of us mixes flush chrome with more than one grounded pane, and a non-flush SIDE pane beside a flush one keeps paying in full for the same reason. **CLOSED 2026-08-29 — a floating pane owes the content what it COVERS, and the frame now says how much** (Kushagra: *"some value, equivalent of the space sidebar is taking including gaps padding etc, to make content act as if sidebar is not floating, so that I can decide what content I want to bleed to edges and be behind sidebar, and what isnt"*). Four lengths on the content pane — `--kui-shell-inset-inline-start` / `-inline-end` / `-block-start` / `-block-end` — each the reach of the panes underlapping that side: the token width plus the gap either side, or one control row plus the pane's padding and its two borders. The app spends them wherever it likes, which is the whole point: pad the reading column and let the photograph bleed. The guidance is unchanged — text respects the coverage, media may ignore it — and it is now expressible.

**The wrinkle recorded above is real and it is answered by measurement rather than by inference.** A pane's `width` prop writes `--kui-shell-w` on the PANE, registered `inherits: false` on purpose, and no sibling can read another element's inline style — so the published reach is the FRAME's own extent, exact for a pane taking what the frame gives it and stale for a pane that overrode its width, height or size. Refusing the override by type was proposed and does not survive: whether anything underlaps a pane depends on `flush` on a DIFFERENT component, which no type can see, so the refusal would have to cover every floating pane including the many where nothing reaches underneath. A development-only guard measures instead — it compares the published length against where the floating panes actually are, on the layout frame and on resize — and the fix it names is to state the extent as `--shell-sidebar-w` on the Shell, where the pane and the content read one number. Reading the props would have been a proxy for the thing that matters and would go quietly wrong the day a new way to change a pane's extent exists.

**The pane grew its own chrome rows the same day — `ShellPaneHeader` / `ShellPaneFooter` (2026-08-29), and the anatomy criterion is answered rather than waived.** The pinned stack stays the default and needs no parts; what forces a name is the FLOATING posture, which the informal stack cannot express: a floating row leaves flow, and the content behind it needs to know its reach — a height only a named part can STATE (one control row at the pane's index plus the pane's padding, ShellHeader's own derivation) rather than measure. In flow the parts still earn their keep by stating the height, so a pane's chrome stands level with the rail beside it at every index. Floating, the part paints NOTHING — its children are the chrome, and a fill would be a second surface identity inside a pane that already has one; it pairs with ScrollArea's `fade`, which is what keeps passing content legible. The reach is published as `--kui-pane-inset-block-start`/`-end` on the pane — the frame's safe-area pattern one level down, spent by the app and never auto-spent — with the pane's own reset for the nested case and the same derived-not-measured staleness (a floating row that outgrows one control row publishes less than it covers). `--kui-shell-row` flipped to `inherits: true` for it: every pane declares the row, so inheritance can never override a pane's own answer, and the flip is what lets a pane's CHILD read the number its floor derives from.

**The lengths are NOT declared on the placement rules beside them, and that difference is the design.** A placement is a no-op when its pane is not there — the track collapses and `sidebar-start` IS `content-start` — so those rules never had to ask whether the pane was standing. A length is simply wrong: a closed sidebar still publishing its width would push every inset region a sidebar's width off the edge of a frame with no sidebar in it. So the reach asks the stricter question, which a closed pane, an overlaying pane, a detail pane resting shut and a narrow window all answer no to. Its guards sit in `:where()` for the reason the floating rule's own do — `:has()` takes its most specific argument's weight, and a chain of four `:not([attr])` would have made these rules heavy enough that the narrow block, which gains nothing from being in a media query, could not overrule them. **One combination is knowingly wrong and stated rather than hidden:** two nav columns where one is `fixed` and the other `auto`, on a narrow window — the narrow rule zeroes the whole side while the fixed column still stands. Nobody builds that frame, and the guard measures it. **A name collapsed on the way:** `--kui-shell-header-row` and `--kui-shell-rail-square` were two names for one number, with the header's own comment saying so out loud, and the content pane pricing a floating header's reach would have been a third — they are `--kui-shell-row`, on the pane, so every pane including the content reads its own.

**Audited the same day (ultracode, 26 agents, one skeptic per finding, every repair re-measured by hand and every new law falsified; LOG carries it in full). Nineteen candidates, fifteen survived, five repairs.** The CRITICAL one predates this change: `max-inline-size: calc(100% - var(--touch-target-min))` on an absolutely positioned grid item resolves its percentage against the item's GRID AREA, and an out-of-flow item does not size its own `auto` track — so every overlaying pane on every narrow window had been rendering **1px wide, `clientWidth` 0**, since the cap was written to stop the opposite defect. Its law was a bound in ONE direction ("a dismissal strip survives") which a 1px pane satisfies happily; a side pane now spans every column and the bottom pane every row, and the law asserts `min(designed, frame − floor)` at both ends. The same repair then shipped HALF-APPLIED for one run — into the explicit overlay arms and not the `auto` restatement, which is the path every phone takes — and the agreement law that exists to hold those two implementations together read four properties, none of them the width, at two different viewports. Two repairs were the posture's own: the floating rule outranked the overlay arms at (0,5,0) vs (0,2,0), because `:has()` takes its most specific argument's weight, so a pane that was both non-flush and overlaying sank into the scrim's band and every press inside the drawer dismissed it (`:where()` now zeroes it; the shipped comment had asserted the arithmetic instead of measuring it). And `grid-column-start: rail-start` asked only whether the RAIL floats, so a flush sidebar standing between it and the content was painted out entirely by the content's own seal — guarded now, and the guard falsified a second sentence of mine in passing (the rail's rule wins on SPECIFICITY, measured with the blocks swapped, not on the source order the comment claimed). Fifth: a glass pane never built the LENS, so the shell was the one glass in the library defended by blur alone. **Recorded, not fixed:** a grounded pane still resolves the theme's glass (the material is decided in JS from `flush` while floating is derived in CSS — a decision, not a defect, and it waits) — **CLOSED 2026-08-29 by making the material a prop, not by teaching JS the derivation: see the glass paragraph above**; and two older material-layer facts that reach every scrolling glass surface rather than only the shell — a flush pane on glass has no single seam (`--kui-border-color` computes transparent there and the ring paints all four sides), and a glass pane's ring scrolls out of it (the pane is a scroll container and the ring is an `::after` at `inset: 0`, laid out in the scrolled coordinate space).

**Passing `open` conditionally is supported and stated (2026-08-21).** `{...(preview ? { open: false } : {})}` pins a pane closed while a flag is on and hands control straight back: the uncontrolled state is left untouched while the controlled value is in force, so the pane returns to where the user last left it rather than to where the pin was. It already worked and nothing said it was allowed, which is how a refactor takes a behaviour away silently. React warns about this shape for form inputs because a value has nowhere to go; a pane's does.

**Recorded open, from the same port: `auto` on the inspector has one resolution and an editor wants the other.** An inspector and a bottom pane rest closed at every width, on the reasoning that detail is asked for — right for a document app, wrong for an editor whose inspector is half the interface, and the porting agent had to hand-write the window-class rule in the app to get "open when roomy, closed on a phone". `defaultOpen` is not that answer: it forces the pane open on a phone too. So the gap is that `auto` names one resolution per pane type with no way to ask for the other, and the likely shapes are a third value on the existing prop or an `auto` that reads what the pane is FOR. Not decided here — it changes the resting state of every inspector in every app, which is Kushagra's call and wants a real screen in front of it.
**A SEAM NEEDS SOMETHING ON THE OTHER SIDE OF IT (2026-08-29, Kushagra: "Say content is not flush, and the sidebar is. Then the separator looks weird, no? Idea is if one is not flush, maybe it shouldn't have separator").** Measured on a 1280 x 600 shell with a flush header, a flush sidebar and a `flush={false}` content: the sidebar's hairline at x=288, the content card's left edge at x=296 — 8px of bare page between them, and the same 8px under the header's hairline. Since a flush pane paints no fill (2026-08-21, below), both sides of that line are page: it is a rule drawn across a continuous stretch of page, a few pixels from the boundary the card has already drawn with its margin, corner and cast. **The condition is the CONTENT, and it falls out of the posture derivation rather than being a list of cases:** when the content is flush every non-flush pane FLOATS and the content grows underneath it, so every seam still meets it (measured — a floating sidebar leaves the rail's hairline and the content's left edge both at x=65, touching); when the content is not flush nothing floats at all, the content is a card inset on four sides, and every seam facing it is left against a gap. **The rail is the one seam that need not face the content** — a flush sidebar standing between them is still in the frame, so rail|sidebar is a genuine boundary and keeps its line; with the sidebar pulled off the frame, or absent, the rail faces the gap like everything else. **The header goes with them, and the consequence is stated rather than hidden** (Kushagra's call, taking "nothing draws against a pulled-away pane" whole): its bottom edge spans the flush columns as well as the content's, so dropping it leaves a header and a flush sidebar as one continuous page-coloured region with no rule between them — a border cannot be drawn along part of an edge, so the alternative was a line right over the sidebar and stray over the card, and a macOS unified toolbar over a sidebar draws no line on the sidebar's side either. **`:where()` around the `:has()` is the whole of the drawer's safety**, and the first spelling got its own reason wrong: it also carried `:not([data-presentation="overlay"])` on every subject with a comment claiming that was the protection, when it in fact raised each rule to (0,3,0) and turned a rank the overlay exception wins outright into a tie settled by source order. The sabotage pass caught it — removing the guards changed nothing, which is what says a guard is decoration — and the law is falsified by removing the `:where()` instead, which lands the stand-down at (0,5,0) and really does zero one border of a drawer. Eight laws, including the flush-frame control without which asserting `0px` proves nothing.

**A FLUSH PANE PAINTS NOTHING — FILL, LIGHT AND CAST — AND A DRAWER IS NOT FLUSH (2026-08-21, Kushagra: "flush should have no background at all, it's flush to page"; "when it becomes a drawer, it stops being flush… a drawer has a surface, so it gets surface").** Two halves of one sentence, and the measurement that opened it: header, sidebar and content in a flush frame all painted the identical `--color-surface`, the same value a Card paints, so the fill said nothing the hairlines were not already saying. Flush already deleted the corner and the border — the two other things that make a box a surface — so the fill was the leftover, and a pane level with the page is not a plane. It stands down through `--kui-sf-fill`, the hook surfaces.css reads first, leaving the material chain under it untouched. **A FILL IS ONLY ONE OF THE THREE THINGS A PLANE DOES, and the first pass stood down only that one** (Kushagra, by eye, on the shipped result: *"why does it still seem to have background?"*). Measured with the fill already gone: a grain-and-bloom `background-image` still painting each pane lighter at its top, and a full `0 1px 2px` + `0 24px 64px -12px` cast — every pane in the frame throwing a surface shadow at its neighbours. Both are what a plane does. The light stands down as the PROPERTY and never as `--kui-sf-light`, which is Ground's own spelling copied deliberately: that hook is not registered `inherits: false`, so setting it on the pane would strip the rim off every card INSIDE it — the var-inheritance trap this system has now been bitten by five times. `--kui-sf-fill` is safe as a hook because it IS registered non-inheriting, checked rather than assumed. The cast stands down on the pane's own non-inheriting name, consulted before the world's inherited chrome, so one declaration closes both hops. **The consequence is stated rather than hidden: a flush shell no longer supplies the app's background.** The page does, which is the app's call in this system and always was — the library paints no page, and the shell filling the window had been papering over the same hazard every unpainted Card already has in dark. In dark the frame moves from the seal to the page, which is a step DOWN and visible; in light the two are within a hair of each other.

**AND THE SEAM HAD TO BECOME A RULE (2026-08-21, Kushagra: "what is not so trivial is separation between shell panes when they are flush… we have used hairline for exactly this and I don't see why we can't use it here also").** The per-side widths were never the problem — each pane draws only its INNER edge, so two neighbours can never double one, and the two boundaries the question names are each owned exactly once (rail|sidebar by the rail's inner edge, sidebar|content by the sidebar's, with the content pane drawing nothing on any side). What went missing was the PIGMENT: `--surface-edge` rests at a live `transparent` in the elevated world on purpose, because there a pane's boundary IS its cast — so once the cast went away that promise had nothing behind it, and the seam measured 1px of nothing in the default world while appearing correctly under `depth="flat"`. **`--color-border` is the answer the system already gives for a rule between regions** — what a Separator draws and what a tab bar's hairline draws, both law-pinned to resolve one colour, and §7's edge order puts a frame seam exactly where a separator sits. Deliberately not `--tone-border`: that is the pane-BOUNDARY role, and a flush pane has no boundary of its own to state. In `flat` the two resolve identically so nothing moves; in `elevated` the seam simply appears. **Stated as the PROPERTY, and the reasoning is the law's own**: a component sheet may not mention the painted variable (the checkbox's audit defect (a)) — a component re-points a role and only the shared layer paints. The role was tried first and leaks, because the surface edge role is not registered `inherits: false` and setting it on the pane hands every card inside a visible edge in the elevated world. The property reaches this box and no other, and the law's spirit is met rather than dodged because the line carries no STATE: a pane has no invalid or disabled arm for a remap to have to reach.

**The drawer half is what makes the first half safe.** `flush` is the app's statement about the FRAME, and a pane sitting over the content is not in the frame while it does so — so it takes the surface identity back, whatever the app asked. Before this a drawer on a phone was a square, borderless slab (measured: corner 0px, border 0px, against 40px and 1px on a pane the app had pulled off the frame), because `[data-flush]` was still stamped and the frame dress still applied; with the fill gone it would have been a see-through one. **No new vocabulary** — nothing floating, nothing stacked, the 2026-08-20 spelling untouched: one exception at (0,3,0), `[data-flush][data-presentation="overlay"]`, plus its restatement for the resolved value in the one media block. Keyed on specificity because the exception's real shape is "flush AND overlaying", and because the first spelling put the declarations inside the position rules, which lose to the flush block below them — the drawer stayed square and the law caught it. **Resolved in CSS, never in JS**: the JS knows a pane is overlaying only after mount, so a server-rendered `defaultOpen` drawer would have painted square for a frame and then corrected, and first paint being right with no script is the whole of this section. **The restore is the SHELL's corner, not the shared layer's** — this file pins every pane to `--radius-surface-3` rather than riding the surface size join, so a first spelling that restated surfaces.css's join expression came out 38.712px against 40; "restore the surface identity" means restore what this component gives a pane off the frame, not what the shared layer would give some other box.

**Pane widths are the system's first sanctioned raw lengths — decided, with the reason stated.** Every prior "give me a number" was refused because a ladder existed to answer it; a pane's width has no ladder to consult — it is a statement about the app's content ("my file tree needs 280px"), the same room-question §13 routes to the app, and no index can price it (the fraction wall says pricing it against any existing ladder would be instance nine). Shape: each pane carries one designed default width in config (raw designed numbers, the `switchW` genus — v0: rail 64, sidebar 288, inspector 320, bottom 200, inherited from v1's judged values), emitted as tokens the stylesheet consumes, so the sheet stays token-only and the budget laws hold. The pane's `width` prop (a number) overrides by writing the SAME custom property inline — one name per pane, which is deliberately the whole future resize architecture: a later drag writes where the prop writes, persistence reloads what drag wrote, and nothing about today's shape is revisited. `minWidth`/`maxWidth` ship with resize, not before.

**THE RAIL IS NOT A THIN SIDEBAR, and its width is not the app's to state (2026-08-20; LOG carries the correction).** Kushagra, after I twice proposed the rail might be the sidebar with its labels off: *"Rail is primary navigation, tools, regions… Rail = high level regions, and panel can appear as a secondary navigation expanding each item in rail. When collapsing, sidebar never converges to rail, sidebar can have many items, rail is intended to have a few."* They coexist — the rail picks, the sidebar shows what was picked — and neither replaces the other. The measurable consequence is the API: the width table's own stated reason ("a pane's width is the app's content speaking") is true of the sidebar, the inspector and the bottom pane and FALSE of the rail, whose width is its item's box plus the air around it. Shipped, it was a flat 64 in both pointer worlds while its contents were 32 fine and 44 coarse — the column did not answer the axis its own contents answer, which is the 2026-08-10 icon-box finding one level up, and the rule had been argued for three panes and applied to a fourth. **So the sidebar takes a `width` and the rail takes a `size`**: `shellWidth` loses its rail entry, the extent derives from `--control-height-N` at the index the pane was given, and `width` is refused by the rail's type. The extent is stated as a CONTENT box, which is the half-pixel the first spelling got wrong: a pane is border-box, so a flush rail's own seam hairline came out of the derivation and the square landed 3.5px from the edge against a designed 4 — and would have moved again the moment the rail stopped being flush and grew four borders instead of one.

**Its item is a SQUARE, and icon-only is the default rather than an option.** A row is icon-then-label with a height of one text line plus air; a rail item has no label beside it, so it is an icon-only control whose box is square by construction — the skeleton's `min-height` and the stated inline size come from one join and cannot disagree, which is why a first spelling's `aspect-ratio: 1` was deleted as dead by its own sabotage. Icon-only is the default because narrow is part of what a rail MEANS: the moment it carries words it is wide enough to read as a small sidebar, which is the confusion this whole entry exists to end. Labels remain available as a switch on the PANE rather than per item (one word under one icon and not the next is how a column of icons stops lining up), and a labelled rail will need its own designed widths — recorded, not built.

**THE PAINT IS INSET; THE TARGET IS NOT (Kushagra: "I think I still need inset on rail items too, like sidebar")** — and it reaches both members, because the mechanism is one shared rule. Apple's sidebar geometry since Big Sur: a Finder row paints a rounded rectangle with a margin either side while the whole row still takes the click. The margin does the paint and `::after` gives the press back the full column, which is §16's expander — the mark family's own mechanism — reused rather than re-invented. The alternative, a row simply narrower than its column, leaves a strip that looks pressable and is not: the paint-versus-target disagreement the checkbox audit already paid for. **The distance is the PANE'S OWN PADDING since 2026-08-21**, and the row's margin is deleted with it: `shellNavInset` was a second number saying what the padding now says, and it existed only because a pane claimed to have none. The expander reads `--kui-sf-p` — the surface padding hook, which inherits on purpose, because that is how `m="bleed"` finds its nearest surface — so the paint's inset and the press that undoes it are one number by construction rather than two kept equal by hand. A second mechanism was caught doing the same job and removed: `align-items: center` on the rail's list landed the square at exactly the offset the inset produces, so a sabotage deleting it survived — one mechanism, and it is the one the expander is measured from.

**Selection speaks the nav row's vocabulary, and the tab strip is REFUSED for now.** `current` is the same two stamps (`data-tone="accent"` + the medium rung), so hover is grey, current is accent, and hovering the current square still moves. I argued in the design conversation that a rail is a tab strip stood on its end, and for VS Code's activity bar that is true — but real tab semantics need `role="tablist"`, roving focus and PANELS, and the panels are the app's, not the shell's. A nav rail navigates, where `aria-current` is correct and `role="tab"` would be wrong. Recorded open: the tablist variant, the day a shell owns the panels it would switch. The bottom run is the deliberate exception either way — account and settings squares open menus and are never current, which is why `ShellRailList` confers layout and never membership.

**The sidebar's own anatomy: one scrolling part, and an optional navigation vocabulary (2026-08-20).** Three parts, and the split is who owns what. **Structure is the shell's**, because the shell is what made its panes scroll: `ShellScroll` marks the ONE region that scrolls, after which everything else pins by being an ordinary-sized child of a column — a pinned header and a pinned footer are not parts, they are siblings. It is a PANE fact rather than a sidebar one (the inspector has Figma's Design/Prototype tabs over a scrolling body; the bottom pane has VS Code's tab strip), so one part serves all of them. It IS a ScrollArea, which is also where half the mechanism already lived: a first spelling repeated `min-block-size: 0` here and a sabotage pass proved the repetition dead, because `.kui-scroll-area` declares it for its own reasons — so the pane contributes the flex and the scroller contributes the automatic-minimum override. **The evidence it was missing was our own builder**, which did not use ShellSidebar at all: it rebuilt the panel by hand with 31 raw `style` escapes, five of them `minHeight: 0`. It moved into the Shell the same day (LOG 2026-08-20), which is also what found the hide defect recorded under "auto until touched" above — being the first consumer to put a ShellScroll in a pane it then asked to close. **Navigation vocabulary is the shell's and OPTIONAL** — `ShellNavGroup` and `ShellNavItem`, used when the content IS navigation, never presented as the only legal content. **The instrument is the app's**: a layers tree is a kookie-block and Kookie ships none of it (Kushagra: *"a shell sidebar with a tree built in is a kookie block, not kookie ui"*), so what the pane owes arbitrary content is a box with a real height, the right region scrolling, alignment with the rows above it, and `m="bleed"` for tree rows that want the pane's full width — which the Card work shipped before this asked for it. *(Amended 2026-08-26: this sentence names the INSTRUMENT — the layers panel, the file browser — and those stay blocks. The tree MACHINE under them is spec'd as the package's in §33; the docs-site entry's "the tree the package will ship" was the other half of the same split, and the two read as a contradiction until §33 reconciled them.)* The group is a part for the non-visual half only (§10's criterion): a heading rendered as a sibling is a heading nobody is told about, so it carries `role="group"` and points `aria-labelledby` at its own label, while its heading keeps the SHORT line-plus-inset box — by its own stand-down since 2026-08-26, when the standing rows around it took the height ladder (§21) — because a caption is not a target. **The heading also carries a WEIGHT STEP (2026-08-25, from the docs site's own sidebar):** it shipped sharing every type property of the rows under it — 14px, weight 400, same case — and differing in ink alone, which is §15's similarity rule inverted, one treatment worn by two roles. Measured on a five-group column it is what made a grouped sidebar read as one undifferentiated list of links. Weight and NOT size, because the heading wears the row skeleton precisely so its words sit on the rows' own left edge: a size step buys the same rank and breaks the alignment the part exists to give. The menu's group label is the same two declarations byte for byte and deliberately did NOT move — a menu's groups are bounded by a panel and by separators, so distance and enclosure already do there what the sidebar asks the label to do alone; when a third group heading appears, that is the promotion, and this is the fact it promotes. Pinned by a law that reads the heading and a row AGAINST EACH OTHER through a mounted Theme, because asserting the heading is `500` passes just as happily on a stylesheet that sent every row to 500. **Size is stated on the PANE and stamped per row** — Menu's shape exactly (§22), because the control size join keys `[data-size]` per element with `inherits: false` cells, and because asking every row would be asking the call site to keep nine answers agreeing. **And it is stated on the SHELL once (2026-08-21, from the builder's port):** each pane defaulted to `"2"` independently, so a size-1 editor said `size="1"` on the sidebar, the rail and the inspector separately and would have found out the day it added a row that a missed pane was silently size 2 — a default with no home, which is the one thing a system with a size axis should not ship. `Shell` takes the index and a pane resolves to it unless it states its own; one context serves both hops (the root provides, a pane re-provides whatever it resolved to, the rows read the same name), so the literal exists in exactly one place. Never the pane's WIDTH — the width table's reason applies unchanged.

**A PANE IS A SURFACE, AND IT PADS (2026-08-21, Kushagra: "all shell panes must have padding all around… consider it as a safe area").** Every pane said `padding: 0` from the day it shipped, with "a pane's content owns its own inset" written beside it, and the result was an app frame with no air in it: measured in apps/docs, the header 28px tall with its buttons against the top edge, nav rows 4px from the pane wall, and three consumers re-deriving the frame's height by hand in four different spellings. The rule was FORCED once and then generalised past its case. A highlighted nav row must span its column and a scroller must reach the pane's edges, so air the ROW cannot escape would break both — but neither needs the PANE to have none, and both escapes already existed elsewhere in the library: a `ScrollArea` that is a direct child of any surface bleeds out to that surface's edge and re-pads its own viewport (§10, the mechanism Card ships), and anything else that wants the whole box says `m="bleed"` (§3, the picture-in-a-card mechanism). So the padding declaration goes, the size join is simply allowed to reach the pane, and the defect that once forced the hook to zero — a `ShellScroll` at x = −16, 319px wide inside a 288px pane — disappears with it: the bleed was never wrong, the pane's claim to have no padding was. **The ladder is Card's**, deliberately not a shell-private one; that it swamps a control-sized pane at the top of the index (an 88px rail at size 3, 112px at size 4, because the padding ladder nearly triples across the index while the control ladder does not quite double) is the fraction wall's shape and is recorded for the eye pass rather than pre-solved. **The pane's CORNER answers the index with everything else**, in the same change: it had been pinned to the default card's step with a stated reason — a shell composed inside another surface must not inherit that ancestor's corner, and `--kui-sf-radius` inherits — and the reason was spent the moment the pane declared the hook itself through the size join, which shadows an ancestor rather than merely out-shouting it. What was left was a pane taking an index that prices its padding, its rows, its rail's width and its header's height and then not its own shape. The declaration goes and `.kui-surface`'s own rule prices it, multiplier included; the drawer's two arms restore the shared layer's EXPRESSION rather than a pinned step, because a corner has to come back as a property there (the flush rule zeroed the property, and `initial` on a property is zero) and the hook cannot be re-pointed instead — a `[data-flush]` arm and the size join's `[data-size]` arm are both (0,2,0) and would be settled by which file loaded first. The law that pinned the old value is rewritten as an AGREEMENT with a mounted Card at the same index, which is what the squircle multiplier obliges (38.712px against 40, measured once already) and what makes the vacuity guard possible: a pinned corner agrees with a Card at exactly one index. **The whole change is a NET DELETION of CSS** — 18 bytes gzipped — which is the evidence it was made at the right layer.

**And a HEADER states its height, which the padding decision answers for free.** A header's box is one control ROW at the pane's index, inside the pane's padding — so a header priced at an index is exactly as tall as the rail at that index is wide, and the app frame's corner is square by construction. It is a FLOOR: something taller grows the header rather than being clipped by it, and something shorter centres in it rather than defining it. Three spellings are load-bearing and each is pinned by a law that fails without it. **Content-box**, the rail's own reason on the other axis: under `border-box` a `min-block-size` of 32 is satisfied by 23px of content plus padding and seam, so the stated row never binds and the header goes straight back to measuring its tallest control — the defect wearing a token. **Grid and not flex** for the centring: a header's usual child is one full-width row, and a flex container makes that child shrink-wrap, so every existing consumer's header would have collapsed to its content. And **the index has to reach the header at all** — `ShellHeader`, `ShellContent` and `ShellBottom` stamped no `data-size`, so nothing priced per index could key on them; all six panes resolve and stamp it now, through one helper rather than four copies of the resolution.

**Deferred, not refused — and the deferrals are load-bearing:** **Resize** (Kushagra: later, but very important) — it is JS at interaction time by definition, so it lands with a written carve-out the way `useWindowClass` earned "the first sanctioned runtime signal", plus the ARIA separator/slider wiring v1 left open, min/max, and persistence; the one-variable width design above is the room left for it. **Peek** (hover-preview of a closed pane) — v1's least load-bearing feature with its highest structural cost (a context slice, absolute overlays, its own z-band, per-pane CSS). **`stacked`** — folded into `flush={false}` on 2026-08-20 rather than shipped under its own name, because it was always the missing half of the posture rather than a third presentation; the **mixed posture** shipped with it.

**Stacking and motion inherit standing law.** Whatever the shell layers internally stays inside its own isolation scope and never competes with portals — §20 names the shell as exactly the ancestor that buries every popup, and the root both isolates and takes no z-index of its own, so a portal outside the theme frame wins on DOM order however the panes stack. (This said "no pane creates a positive z-index" until 2026-08-20, when a floating pane started carrying one permanently; the law that should have caught the drift mounted an all-flush shell, where the sentence cannot be false — the fixture, not the assertion, is what could not fail.) Motion is the recorded follow-up and is NOT shipped: shell.css declares zero transitions and a node law asserts the absence (this paragraph stated the pane motion in the present tense for one commit, two paragraphs above the sentence saying it ships none — caught by three lenses of the 2026-08-16 audit, and the shortest doc-code drift in this file since the alert's padding). When it lands it takes the two clocks: geometry (the width) on the recovery spring, content legibility as paint, reduced motion standing down a hook (v1 blanket-killed transitions in one media block — the pre-2026-08-10 shape this system already replaced). The root never paints `100vh` by assumption (v1 hardcoded a viewport height behind a `[style*='height']` sniff); height is the app's, with `100dvh` as the documented default posture, not a baked rule.

**Shipped 2026-08-16, same day as the spec — repetition's eleventh entry, +556 bytes gzipped for the whole frame** (tokens included: four designed extents, the gap pick per density scope, and a stylesheet that is almost entirely geometry because every pane is a `.kui-surface` and paints nothing of its own). The mechanism that carried the build: the frame is ONE grid whose areas the panes claim for themselves (`grid-area` by name — placement without child inspection, DOM order stays reading order, a missing pane's `auto` track collapses to zero), and the not-touching posture's even spacing was HALF the gap on the frame's padding plus half on every pane's margin, equal by construction at every pane combination including the absent ones (a grid `gap` was rejected because an empty track still bills its two sides — the doubled-edge defect v1 had, by other means). **That construction is now scoped to the all-cards frame**, which is the one regime it is exact in: the 2026-08-20 rewrite proved algebraically that no per-pane constant can also hold a mixed boundary, so a non-flush pane pays in full instead — see the posture paragraph above. The overlay treatment is the same grid item leaving flow: an absolutely positioned grid child resolves its insets against its own GRID AREA, which is what puts an overlaying sidebar exactly under the header row with no measured arithmetic. Its obligations shipped whole — scrim (dialog's recipe), Escape, the rest of the shell `inert` (focus AND pointer AND the accessibility tree), focus into the pane and back to the trigger — and **containment is the ROOT's, not the pane's, which is the 2026-08-16 audit's repair rather than the first cut's design**: see the audit paragraph below. Two decisions closed in the building and recorded in LOG: an untouched pane with EXPLICIT overlay presentation rests closed at every width (an overlay is summoned, never ambient — without this, `presentation="overlay"` plus nobody-touched-anything opened a scrimmed overlay at first paint on desktop), and flush seams are single hairlines by each pane drawing only its INNER edge (border-width overrides that leave the border's colour to the surface system, so `contrast="high"` reaches a flush seam exactly as it reaches a card's edge (this said "the look axis and" until the merge with main, where that axis was deleted)). MOTION IS THE RECORDED FOLLOW-UP on Menu's own precedent (shipped instant, moved next day): shell.css declares zero transitions, a node law asserts that absence, and the spring entry above is the next session's work. 33 laws (27 mounted, 6 node), the load-bearing five falsified against sabotaged code before being trusted — and one sabotage was itself caught being a no-op (a replace whose target string never matched reported a green suite as proof; sabotages assert their own application now). 1,335 package tests, 155 docs tests.

**Audited the same day (ultracode, 17 agents — eight lenses, eight skeptics, one completeness critic; every finding re-measured in a mounted browser). 44 raised, 33 survived refutation, deduping to seven repairs — and the headline is that the shell could permanently brick an application.** Four lenses found it independently, by pointer alone, with no controlled props: **containment was written per pane**, so with two overlays live each pane inerted its sibling — including a sibling that was itself a live overlay, leaving two visible drawers unfocusable, out of the accessibility tree and excluded from hit-testing — and each pane's snapshot recorded values the other had already written, so whichever cleanup ran last restored `inert = true` onto `<header>` and `<main>`. The app was then non-operable, keyboard and pointer, until reload. The shipped comment had priced exactly this case as "two overlays closed out of order would restore a beat early (accepted: the scrim closes all of them)"; it was not a beat early, it was forever, and the scrim — the mitigation that sentence named — was the path that produced it. **All 33 laws were green throughout, because every overlay law in the file mounted exactly ONE live overlay** (the fixture's other panes resolve `auto` → closed at narrow): the repo's own "a law about one axis of a two-axis mechanism is half a law", one axis being *how many*. The repair is structural, not a patch: containment, focus capture and Escape are the ROOT's, in one pass with ONE lazily-filled snapshot, exempting the scrim and the root child **containing** each live overlay — containing, not equal to, because a pane the consumer wrapped in a plain `<div>` is not a root child and identity-matching inerted the wrapper, so the shell inerted its own open drawer (the critic's find, and the worst kind: a reasonable consumer shape). The pass runs on every Shell render rather than on registry notifications alone, because an ordinary child mounted behind the scrim notifies nothing and was left fully live. Escape moved from `document` to the root element — a document listener is layer-blind, so a Dialog opened inside an overlaying pane dismissed the pane underneath with its own Escape.

Six more repairs, each falsified against the pre-fix code: **the overlay caps its extent** (uncapped, an overlaying pane measured the window exactly at 320px — scrim 0px wide, every hit-test returning the pane, no pointer route back at all;
