every arm now caps at `calc(100% - var(--touch-target-min))`, because the strip IS the tap that closes it, and the same declaration stops an oversized `width` prop pushing document scroll); **`box-sizing: border-box` on the frame** (the floating posture spends half its gap as padding, which under content-box was added OUTSIDE the declared `100%` — a floating shell in a 600px box rendered 608 tall, the block-end gap read zero against its container, and the documented `100dvh` posture gained a scrollbar); **the header bounds its inline axis** (`overflow-inline: clip`, block axis still visible for the focus ring — a header whose content outgrew it pushed document-level horizontal scroll, so the app frame stopped bounding the app); **`--kui-shell-w/-h` registered `inherits: false`** (the `--kui-h` trap, §12: a sidebar handed its width to every descendant, and a Shell composed inside a pane sized its panes from the outer pane's prop); plus the mirror and plural laws below. **PLACEMENT: the root is what it is designed for, and it must also COMPOSE (2026-08-16, Kushagra: "Shell should be able to sit at app root, its designed for it, or be placed in a modal or dialog or whatever too").** That resolves what the audit had left as an open question about containment's reach, by observing that the reach is correct in both sanctioned placements for different reasons. **At the app root, containing every child of the shell IS containing the app** — there is nothing else — and a law now asserts that the contained set includes app-owned children, not merely panes, so the claim is scoped rather than vague. **Inside a Dialog (or any layer that traps), the enclosing layer owns everything outside the shell**, so the shell containing its own children is exactly its share of the work and duplicating the trap would be wrong. Measured, that placement was already correct in layout and containment — and had exactly one defect: **one Escape closed the pane AND the dialog around it**, the audit's layer-blindness in the opposite direction. The pane is the innermost dismissible thing the user is in, so it answers the key and stops it (`stopPropagation`, never `preventDefault` — this is a dismissal, not a cancelled default, and the flag is what other layers read). Both placements are law-pinned, falsified. The residual case is a shell embedded as a WIDGET in a larger page that traps nothing — there the surrounding page stays tabbable behind the pane, the pointer half is still covered by the scrim, and that is a placement the component does not claim. **Still open and honest:** containment is a post-mount obligation, so a server-rendered `defaultOpen` overlay paints its scrim (pure CSS) but is uncontained for keyboard and AT until hydration — the same honest-null shape as §18, stated rather than implied. Note also that `auto` answers the WINDOW, which is right for the root and is not a container query: an embedded shell should state `presentation` and `open` explicitly rather than inherit an answer about a window it does not fill.

**Five laws were added and every one was falsified against the code before the repair**: the plural (two live overlays stay mutually operable; closing both leaves no child inert), the wrapped pane, the late-mounted child, layer-aware Escape, the overlay caps at two widths, and the frame's containment measured against its container rather than itself. **The mirror finally has laws of its own** — the audit proved by sabotage that breaking the JS mirror's explicit-overlay arm left all 33 green while an untouched `presentation="overlay"` pane reported `aria-expanded="true"` and inerted the whole shell at desktop width, because every law read `display`, which CSS answers alone. Two more of my own laws were caught being unfalsifiable *while writing the repair*: the `inherits: false` law read a 120-character window that spanned into the neighbouring registration and reported ITS value (the "measures the axis that was already right" shape, committed inside the law written to catch it), and the floating-containment law's first spelling re-parented its subject after mounting, so a percentage height resolved against the wrong box and it measured 336px of "gap". Both now read through the loud extractor and an in-tree container. 38 mounted + 8 node laws; budget 25,883 → 25,937 gzipped.

## 28. Field (spec'd 2026-08-21)

**The unit that makes one input make sense.** A control on its own is a box: it does not carry its own name, it cannot say what it is for, and it cannot say what went wrong. Field supplies those three and wires them to the control so assistive technology reads them as one thing.

**It passes §10's anatomy criterion, and by the widest margin of anything in the library.** Three non-visual forcers stack: label association by id, `aria-describedby` for the description, and the announced error. Nothing about the arrangement is a visual preference; every part exists because a platform contract demands a node to point at. This is AlertDialog's licence (§25) with more forcers, not fewer.

**Why now.** The package ships eight input controls — TextField, TextArea, Select, Checkbox, Radio, Switch, Slider, SegmentedControl — and not one of them can be labelled by the system. A caller writes a raw `<label for>` or reaches past us into Base UI. Meanwhile nine files in the package already read Base UI's `Field.Root` state: `recipes.css`'s disabled arm exists *because* `Field.Root` computes disabled at the control, and the invalid remap reads its `data-invalid`. We consume the primitive everywhere and own no API for it, which is §1's stance ("Base UI behind a Kookie surface") failing in the one place it is load-bearing.

**Anatomy: five exports** — `Field`, `FieldItem`, `FieldLabel`, `FieldDescription`, `FieldError`.

**`FieldControl` is deliberately not exported.** Base UI's `Field.Control` is a generic `<input>` for callers who have no other input. Every Kookie control already *is* a Base UI input, and Base UI's inputs read `Field.Root` context by themselves — the id, the label target, the description target, and `data-invalid` all arrive without being passed. So a Kookie control dropped inside a Field wires itself, and a re-export would be a second spelling of the control that is already there. Same call as `Progress.Track` and the Tabs indicator: structure, not API.

**Order: label, control, description, error** (amended 2026-08-21 — see the reversal below). The label sits directly on the control it names, and everything that is *about* that control pools underneath it. The description comes before the error so that an error can arrive without moving anything already on screen. Both show; the error never replaces the description, because removing the instruction at the exact moment the person failed to follow it is the wrong trade.

**The description was above the control for one day, and the reversal is a retracted analogy rather than a change of taste.** The first spelling read §29's rule one level down — *instruction before the act, diagnosis after* — and put the description above. Kushagra reversed it the same week on sight, and the argument that survives scrutiny is that the analogy was over-extended. §29's rule orders things a person meets **at different moments**: a question asked before an act, a report made after one. A field is met **all at once** — it is one unit taken in at a glance, not a sequence walked through — so the only thing vertical position settles inside it is *proximity*, which is what sits beside what. The pairing that has to survive a form of ten fields is the label on its control, and a description between them puts the control two steps from its own name. §29's rule is untouched at the level it was written for; what is retracted is applying it inside a single unit.

The alternative arrangement was weighed: the error directly under the control, with the description last. It puts the more urgent message closest to the thing it is about, and it was rejected because an arriving error then displaces the description — a line moving under the person's eyes at the moment they are being told something. Description first is the stable one, and it reads as rule-then-violation ("Eight digits, no spaces." / "That is four digits short.").

**The recorded cost, which GREW with this change.** GOV.UK puts the hint *above* the input, and their argument is a perception one rather than a preference: under a screen magnifier or at high zoom the focused input fills the view and anything below it may not be there. That cost used to fall on the error alone; it now falls on the description as well, and this paragraph is where that is said rather than buried. Two things bound it. It falls on sighted low-vision users specifically — the description is wired by `aria-describedby`, so a screen reader announces it with the control from any position, and the wiring is what carries the meaning. And every other system a person is likely to have used puts it there: Material, MUI, Polaris, Atlassian, Ant and Chakra all place supporting text below the control, with GOV.UK the notable exception. If a measurement shows the cost biting, the order moves, and this records that it was a knowing trade.

**Layout is stacked, always, and horizontal is refused with a precedent.** A horizontal field is not a direction flip: the label aligns to the control's first line, and the error has to sit under the *control* rather than under the label, which makes it a designed grid with its own rules. That is verbatim the shape of Slider's `orientation` refusal (§11, 2026-08-06) — vertical ships as its own designed set the day something forces it — so `orientation` is refused here on the same terms. `render` stays open, so a caller who wants a grid brings one.

**`size` is Field's, and it reaches the control.** Field prices its own label, description and error, and supplies the index to the control by CONTEXT, with an explicit prop on the control winning. That is the material mechanism (§10, 2026-08-16) reused rather than invented. The alternative — Field pricing only its own text — leaves a size-4 input under a size-2 label with no way to state the pairing once, which is what a person means when they type `<Field size="2">`.

**This is the riskiest half of the component and it is named as such.** Silent inheritance is the `--kui-h` collision, the veil-inheritance defect and the two `@property inherits: false` repairs after it — four instances of one trap. The context supply gets falsified before it is trusted. If it does not survive, the fallback is Field pricing only its own text and the control keeping its own index, and that fallback is API-compatible.

**What it draws is almost nothing:** a column and a gap. Label, description and error are `Text` at roles that already exist (`--color-text`, the muted role, and the destructive ink). The disabled and invalid arms already reach the control through the shared layer. The work here is wiring and order, not paint.

**`Form` is deferred, not refused.** Base UI's `Form` does something the `<form>` element does not: it takes a server error map, distributes it to fields by name, and moves focus to the first invalid one. That is non-visual behaviour, the same forcer that licenses Field's anatomy. It is purely additive and changes nothing about Field's API, so it ships when something has server errors to distribute rather than speculatively.

**`FieldItem` ships 2026-08-21, and it closes a hole the mark family has carried since Checkbox.** A `Field` names ONE control. A checkbox group or a radio group is several controls under one name, and each of them needs a name of its own — "Express" has to be able to say "next business day" without the group's label having anywhere to put it. Base UI's `Field.Item` opens a fresh naming scope, so a label written inside one names the mark standing beside it rather than the group, and a description written inside one is **added** to the ones the field already supplies: an option ends up described by the field's description and then by its own, which is the reading a person would give them anyway. Measured, not assumed — the two-option probe is what proved the scoping, because with one option a correctly scoped label and an implementation where every label resolves to the first control in the field are indistinguishable.

**What it closes:** Checkbox and Radio have always refused to draw their own label (§11 — a mark sits BESIDE its label, so the row owns the space between them and the mark cannot own the row). That refusal was right and it left nobody holding the row. Until now every named checkbox in this repo — in the examples, in the playground, in the forms chapter — was a hand-written `id` + `htmlFor` pair at the call site, which is the exact wiring Field exists to remove. The refusal stands; the row now has an owner.

**The layout is a two-column grid and that is the whole design.** The mark holds the first column; the name and the explanation stack in the second, which is what puts the explanation under the WORDS rather than under the mark. The mark needs no vertical alignment rule, because `--mark-N` IS `--line-height-N` (§4): a mark is exactly one line of its label tall and lands on the label's first line by construction — the same identity that lets a mark grow on a phone with nothing designed twice. The column gap is `--layout-space-3`, the distance every hand-rolled checkbox row in this repo already used. The rows inside an item are one step tighter than the field's own rhythm, and that second distance is the one place §28 allows one: it is *rhythm* and not grouping (§15's own "a section at 6 holding groups at 5"), because a list of options whose insides breathe as widely as the gaps between them is one undifferentiated stack.

**The field's column flipped to `align-items: stretch` to make items work, and the general rule is better than the one it replaced.** The first spelling was `flex-start` with `.kui-control` opting back in, which held only while a field contained exactly one control: a group primitive sitting between the field and its marks is neither a `.kui-control` nor a part we can name, so it shrink-wrapped and every option's explanation wrapped early. Everything spans now and the LABEL is the one exception — stating the exception is the general rule, where enumerating the things that span is a list a caller can always add to. The label is excepted in both places (the field's column and the item's second column) for §16's reason: a label filling the row makes a hit target out of the empty space beside the words.

**One thing it breaks, recorded rather than papered over.** When a field's controls all sit inside items, the field's OWN label renders a `<label for>` pointing at nothing. Base UI's `Field.Root` mints a control id up front and only re-points it when a control registers; items capture those registrations in their own scope, so the outer one keeps an id that never lands. Measured: the id resolves to no element. What it costs is bounded and was checked rather than assumed — the group is still named correctly, because Base UI puts `aria-labelledby` on the `radiogroup` pointing at that label, so nothing is announced wrong; what remains is an HTML validity error and a group label that does nothing when clicked, which no platform makes clickable anyway. What it would take to fix is why it is not fixed: the outer label renders *above* the items, so it cannot read a context they set, and the only automatic mechanism is a state lift that changes the element after mount — which is a hydration mismatch on every server-rendered page. A prop would make a structural fact a per-call-site opinion. Base UI ships the honest escape (`nativeLabel={false}` plus a non-`<label>` element) and it needs the same detection we do not have. Left open, with the measurement, so that whoever closes it does not first have to re-discover it. Note the shape it replaces is not obviously better: **without** items the group's label points at the *first radio*, so clicking the group's name selects an option — actively wrong rather than merely inert.

**SHIPPED 2026-08-21 — repetition's twelfth entry, and +28 bytes gzipped for the whole component**, which is the additivity curve's strongest point to date (Blockquote's +27 was for two declarations; this is six, and it buys a four-part component). The number is what the design predicted: the parts are `Text` with a step, a weight and an ink role, so `field.css` names no colour, no index and no family, and everything about how a field READS arrives from a type layer that was already paid for.

**The gap is one distance and not two,** and the argument is §15's rather than a taste call. A label and its description are one group and the error belongs to the control, so the obvious move is two distances that separate the groups — and this system carries grouping with SIZE and the ink roles. The description is already a step of muted text under a medium label; a second gap says the same thing twice. `--layout-space-3`, v0 for the eye pass like every other distance.

**`align-items: stretch`, with the LABEL as the one exception** (corrected 2026-08-26 — this paragraph stated the superseded first spelling, `flex-start` with the control opting back in, which the paragraph above already records being flipped). A stretched label would make a hit target out of the empty room beside the words, which is §16's own sentence about what a target is, so the label alone is `align-self: start` and is selected by its tag. Everything else spans, because a field's width IS the control's width — and stating the EXCEPTION is what makes that general. The `.kui-control` opt-in it replaced held only while a field contained exactly one control: a group primitive between the field and its marks is neither a `.kui-control` nor a part we can name, so it shrink-wrapped and every option's explanation wrapped early.

**18 mounted laws, eight sabotage passes, every one falsified before it was trusted.** The load-bearing four are the size supply in both directions (measured against a MOUNTED TWIN rather than a token, because the claim is "the same as a control that stated it itself"), the geometric order (read as rectangles, not document order — `column-reverse` keeps the DOM right and puts the words in the wrong place), and the label's step read against the control's own rather than against a number.

**Two of the laws were wrong before they were right, and both failed the way this repo's audits keep describing.** The no-leak law mounted its subject under hostile axes (`dark` + `compact`) and compared it against a twin under the default theme, so it was measuring compact-versus-default and not supplied-versus-unsupplied — a law's two sides must differ in the ONE thing it is about. And the destructive-ink vacuity guard compared the error's colour against `var(--color-text)` resolved INSIDE the error, which can never fail: a stamped tone re-scopes the tone-less role names onto the family's ink trio (§15), so `--color-text` there IS the destructive ink and the guard was comparing a value with itself. It reads against the label now.

**One environment fact worth recording, because it has now bitten twice:** `@base-ui/react/field` had to join the `optimizeDeps.include` list in `vitest.config.ts`. An entry discovered mid-run is optimized in a second pass and holds a different React than the page, so every hook inside it reads off `null` — the config's own comment names `@base-ui/react/input` doing exactly this the first time TextField mounted, and Field reproduced it verbatim.

---

## 29. Messages: what the app tells the person (spec'd 2026-08-21)

**The family is every statement the system makes about state or about the result of an act** — not content an author wrote, not data. It was reconciled whole before any of it was built, because the industry ships four overlapping names for it (callout, alert, banner, toast) and the overlap is §2's inversion: names and appearances first, function last.

**Two questions generate the entire family.** What is the message about, and what made it appear. Answer both and the placement, the lifetime, whether it takes layout space, whether it can be dismissed and its ARIA role are all forced. Nothing else is a free choice.

| About | Appeared because | Component | Space | Lifetime | Dismiss | Action | Role |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| one control | the person's input | `FieldError` (§28) | in flow | until valid | no | none | `alert` |
| a region, or the app | a condition became true | **Notice** | takes space | while true | optional | one, resolving | `status` |
| a decision that blocks | the person's act | `AlertDialog` (§25) ✅ | modal | until answered | no | two | `alertdialog` |
| an act that is over | the person's act | **REFUSED** | — | — | — | — | — |
| the document | an author wrote it | **Aside** (deferred, §15) | in flow | forever | no | none | none |

### Notice

**It is about the region it sits above, it exists while a condition is true, and the person did not cause it.** The specimen it was designed from is Claude Code's usage banner: *"Approaching weekly usage limit · Resets at 1:30 PM · [Get more usage] · ✕"*, sitting directly on the composer it constrains.

**Placement is the caller's** — §3's non-negotiable, applied to a component that every peer lets own its position. In flow above the affected region; the Shell hosts the app-level one. This is exactly why the component is not called Banner. Atlassian needs Banner *and* SectionMessage as two components because their split is where it sits (banner = the whole product, pinned top; section message = above the affected area). Ours is one component and the parent places it.

**It takes layout space and never floats.** The condition is true now, so overlaying it would let it cover content the person needs to see. That single property is what makes it structurally incapable of being the same component as the thing it replaced.

**Two verbs, and they are not the same kind of thing.** At most ONE action that RESOLVES the condition, and a dismissal that only ACKNOWLEDGES it. "Get more usage" raises the limit; "✕" agrees to stop being told about it.

**Dismissal is the app's state, never the component's.** `onDismiss`, no internal `useState`. If the ✕ is forgotten on reload, the ✕ lied — and a component that owns the memory cannot help lying, because it does not outlive the page.

**An icon slot, empty-safe** (Kushagra, 2026-08-21). §8 ships no icon set and apps/docs uses Hugeicons at stroke 1.5. The slot exists because a repeated symbol carries shared meaning across notices in a way a sentence cannot, and a caller with no icon set renders nothing rather than a hole.

**Tone is the category, not the volume.** Neutral is the default and the specimen is grey while being a warning — Apple's "nonintrusive label" reasoning, and §11's existing rule for status surfaces held rather than widened. This is where the semantic families minted 2026-08-05 (success/warning/info) finally get a consumer; they have paid for nothing until now.

**One notice per anchor.** Two stacked notices means neither is read.

**A notice with no action and no dismissal is a `Text`.** Do not ship a box to hold a sentence, and say so in the docs rather than hoping.

**SHIPPED 2026-08-21 — repetition's thirteenth entry, +81 bytes gzipped.** The root is a `.kui-surface` at the tone-forward rung, so the fill, the corner, the padding, the edge and the foreground context all arrive from a layer that was already paid for; `notice.css` is the ROW and nothing else. It names no colour, no size index, no rung and no family — and it declares no `position` and no `z-index`, which is the design rather than an omission: a mounted law reads `static`, `auto`, `none`, because that absence is the whole difference between this component and the one that was refused.

**The anatomy is three slots and no parts.** The symbol, the message, the action and the dismissal, with nothing non-visual forcing a title or a description — the role sits on the root and the two verbs are slots the caller fills. A notice that needs a heading and paragraphs is a Card.

**The dismissal is system-drawn, and Dialog's refusal of a ✕ does not bind here (§24).** That refusal was priced on a panel with two other ways out — Escape and an outside press — where a corner glyph competes with the one action zone. A notice has neither: the ✕ IS the only route out, its position is the trailing edge by convention, and requiring every call site to compose an icon-only Button means every call site re-derives the accessible name. So `onDismiss` renders a real Button with a name, the glyph drawn on the system's own 16-grid at 1.5 stroke like the tick and the carets, and `dismissLabel` is the one place an app that does not speak English states its word.

**`--kui-sf-icon` joins the surface size join** (§10, §12), published from the SAME ladder `--kui-ct-icon` reads, so a symbol on a surface and a symbol in a button at one index are one box rather than two that drift. It sits in the shared layer for Dialog's own reason: a per-size spelling belongs there, and the no-axis walk forbids `data-size` in a component stylesheet outright.

**Three things were wrong on the day it shipped, found by measuring the component rather than re-reading its source, and fixed the same day (Kushagra's calls).**

**It cast a Card's shadow byte-for-byte, and it should cast nothing.** §5's plane criterion has been the answer twice already — elevation dresses boxes that establish a plane of their own, which is why the field family left the elevated world twice and why a ground stands its cast down with "a hole in a plane throws no shadow". A notice is a marker ON a plane: a strip inside a card, or across a page. It shipped casting because it wears `.kui-surface` and nobody asked. Stated now on the pane's own non-inheriting name, with a mounted law whose NEGATIVE CONTROL is a Card in the same world — without that control, deleting the surface chrome outright would pass. **The pool deliberately survives**: on glass it is the seat-line the material HAS, not the lift the app SAYS, which is §10's own split.

**The material never reached it.** Measured, a notice under a glass theme computed `backdrop-filter: none` and stamped nothing while a `<Card backdrop>` beside it drew the lens. The DEFAULT was right — selectivity means an in-flow strip with nothing behind it resolves solid and pays nothing — but there was no way to say otherwise, so a notice pinned over a scrolling region could never express what the app is built of. It takes `backdrop` now and wires it exactly as Card does, GlassScope included, so a glass strip scopes its subtree and its action button never paints a second veil.

**The words did not grow with the box** — 54px to 114px of strip around 16px of text at every index, while the dismiss button the component places DID scale, so half the contents moved and half did not. The rule being applied was real (a surface never sizes the type inside it) and the exception was already written: type the SYSTEM OWNS. **A notice is an alert that does not interrupt** (Kushagra) — one sentence in a closed arrangement, not a composition somebody built — so the index reaches the message, and it does so through the ALERT's own step map, shared so the two cannot drift. A caller who states a step on their own `<Text>` still wins.

**That promotion moved the map on its third consumer.** `OVERLAY_TITLE_STEP`/`OVERLAY_BODY_STEP` lived in `system/floating.tsx` while both consumers were floating panels; a notice floats over nothing, so a step imported from the floating layer would misname where the rule comes from. They are `OWNED_TITLE_STEP`/`OWNED_BODY_STEP` in `system/type-steps.ts` now, because the rule is ownership and the names should say so.

**The corner was measured and left alone** (Kushagra: "radius is fine"): at the default level it runs 38.7px at size 1 against a 54px strip and 77.4px at size 4 against 114px, so the browser clamps it and a notice reads as a lozenge. That is the surface band doing what it was designed to do; whether a short wide strip wants its own band is not this component's question to answer alone.

**16 mounted laws, eight sabotage passes** — and **one sabotage SURVIVED on the first run, which is the finding worth keeping.** The law that the verbs are not pushed off the end by a long word used `cannot-reach-storage-eu-west-1-replica-seven.internal.example.test` as its fixture, and a browser breaks after every hyphen: the string wrapped on its own, so removing the flex floor changed nothing and the law proved nothing. With a genuinely unbreakable word the same sabotage overflows by 277px. That is the degenerate-fixture rule (2026-08-20) caught in a component built the day after it was written down: **a law over the general case needs an input where the general case and the special case give different answers.**

**Then it happened a SECOND time in the same component, on the same day, in a law written by the author of that sentence.** "A glass strip scopes its subtree" mounted a notice with an action button and asserted the button paints no veil — which passes with the scope deleted, because a button with no `backdrop` of its own and no region around it resolves solid either way. The fixture never created the condition the mechanism defends against. Wrapped in a `<Box backdrop>` region the same sabotage paints `url(#kui-lens-2) blur(2px) saturate(…)` on the button. Twice in one component is the argument for making the question routine: **before trusting a law, ask what the fixture would look like if the mechanism were absent — and if the answer is "the same", the fixture is the defect.**

### Toast is REFUSED (2026-08-21, Kushagra)

**The rule: if an action deserves attention, it gets that attention BEFORE the action. If it does not, we do not narrate it afterwards.** A toast occupies a category that does not exist — important enough to say, too late to act on.

**Both cases argued in its favour were examined and both collapse.** *Undo*: a toast's Undo is a four-second window that resembles reversibility. An app with a real undo stack has it in the menu and on ⌘Z for as long as the history lives, and an app without one is being lent a silhouette instead of a mechanism. Apple does not need toasts because Apple owns undo at the OS level — shake on iOS, ⌘Z on Mac — and can promise it in every app; the web cannot, and the honest answer is to build undo, not to rent its shape. *Copy to clipboard*: the operation genuinely has no visible result, but the best answer is the control reporting its own outcome in place — a copy button showing a done state, where the eyes already are — which is not a toast and is better on every axis.

**Apple's Alerts page is the source of the rule's shape, and it cuts against the naive reading of it:** *"Avoid displaying alerts for common, undoable actions, even when they're destructive… In comparison, when people take an uncommon destructive action that they can't undo, it's important to display an alert."* Apple neither asks nor announces for the ordinary case. It asks first, and only for the rare irreversible one.

**The refusal owes three things, and they are obligations rather than options.** Notice must cover conditions nobody triggered (connection lost, an export ready). A control must be able to report its own outcome briefly — a Button done state, recorded open below. And the stance is written into the docs: if you want reversibility, build undo; the system will not lend you a disappearing window that resembles it.

**One case the refusal does not yet answer, found in our own app while sweeping the docs for drift.** apps/docs' builder ships a local toast, and all four of its messages are the same shape: *"Nothing here accepts that"*, *"That is already resolved"*, *"That file holds nothing this system can place"*, *"That file is not a builder document"*. None is a receipt. Every one is the system **refusing an act**, with a reason, and no field to hang the reason on. The before/after rule does not reach it, because the refusal *is* the answer and cannot be given in advance of the attempt.

The likely answers are outside this family and follow the same logic the rest of it does. Where the refusal is knowable during the gesture, state it during the gesture — a drop target that shows no indicator and a cursor that says no has already said it *before* the act, which is the rule satisfied rather than escaped. Where it is only knowable on release and carries a reason worth reading, that is either a Notice on the surface that refused, or an AlertDialog if it must be acknowledged. Apple's answer for the plain case is the object itself: a file springs back to where it came from, and the snap-back is the message — the same sentence as "the row disappearing is the confirmation", one gesture over.

**Left open on purpose, and the builder's toast is left standing until it is closed** — removing it is a behaviour change to a shipped app and belongs in its own change, not in a taxonomy commit. Recorded here so the refusal is not quietly incomplete.

### The background job is not a message, and solving it here would be the hammer

Raised by Kushagra from Womp, 2026-08-21: people miss background downloads. **A message is an event; a job is an object with a lifecycle, and its artefact outlives the job.** Representing something that persists with something that evaporates is a category error, not a tuning problem.

"People miss it" is five different misses — did it start, is it stuck, did it finish, where is the result now, what did I export before — and only the third is a notification problem at all. The fourth is usually the one that hurts.

**The operating systems solved this with a LOCATION, not an announcement:** a fixed affordance in the frame that carries aggregate state and opens a list (browser downloads, the Dock's progress bar, Finder's copy window). That inverts the property that makes a toast fail — a toast is missable by design because it leaves whether you looked or not, and a chip waits. **The real repair is usually the data model: if the result is durable, missing the moment costs nothing**, and the announcement stops carrying weight the model should carry. The one miss no in-flow affordance can close — the person switched tabs — belongs to the browser's Notifications API, outside this library entirely.

What the system would owe for it: a frame affordance for activity, a Chip carrying a count, and a Popover to hang the panel from. Progress and the row family (§21) already exist. **This is the first forcing case Chip has had** since it was written into §11.

The message family's only part in it is a Notice on the surface where the result changes what the person is currently doing. Never one per job, and never stacking.

### Two names die

`Callout` describes paint, and it is the one name three unrelated things share — an authored aside, a condition notice and an act's receipt. That is §2's inversion in a single word, and it is why every design system has one: a docs site is every design system's first consumer, and the docs site wants an aside. `Banner` names a position, and components in this system do not own their position (§3). `Alert` is taken twice over, by AlertDialog and by the assertive ARIA role. **Notice** names the thing itself.

---

---

## 30. Composer (spec'd 2026-08-23)

**The box a person types a message into.** One text area that grows, a row of controls under it, an optional strip of attachments above it, and one button that sends. It is the input half of a conversation. It is not the conversation.

**It is a conversation component, not an AI component.** shadcn names support inboxes, team threads and group chats in the same sentence as AI chat, and they are the same shape. v1's Chatbar named AI chat as its purpose in the docs, and that framing is part of why it grew an expand/collapse mode and a `sendMode` prop that nothing else has.

**Why it is a surface and not a control.** The deciding question is what it holds. A TextField holds shrunken controls in its slots — there is a whole rule for squashing them to fit and for capping their hit area at the container (§4). A composer holds full-size Buttons at their own index. That is a box containing controls, which is what a Card is.

Three things follow and all of them are already built. Its inset is surface padding, which it has to be, because the attachment strip wants to reach the pane edges and `m="bleed"` only reads `--kui-sf-p` (§3, 2026-08-20). Its corner comes from the surface band, which is right because a composer is not on the height ladder — it grows, the way a TextArea does — and the control band holds a fraction of a height the component does not have, which is the checkbox-corner mistake (§6). And the squircle arrives with the surface band.

**The strongest objection, answered.** The field family already carries every state a composer needs — the ring, disabled, read-only — so making the composer a surface looks like re-deriving the field's state machine one layer up, which is this project's most-repeated fault. It is not a duplicate, for two reasons. A composer has no `invalid` state: a composer is not validated, the app refuses the send. And its ring is not the field's ring — a field's ring is keyed to its own input holding focus, and a composer's has to be keyed to its text area while ignoring the buttons standing inside it, which is the same narrowing rather than a second one. What is left is a ring and a dead state, which is two arms, not a machine.

### What the surface layer owes it

**The disabled arm shipped 2026-08-22 and did NOT apply — corrected 2026-08-23 (Kushagra: "we fixed this bug in card too, card can read disabled, why did we use text field rules?").** The paragraph here used to say it applied unchanged and that its selector "widens by one entry", and the widening never happened; the sentence was written from the arm's INTENT — a dead surface goes grey — rather than from the elements it names. **Two things missed, and only one of them was a defect.**

The PANE misses because the arm is `.kui-surface:where(button, a, label:has(.kui-control))` and a composer is a `<form>`. That is correct as it stands: a disabled TextArea's background is byte-identical to a live one, so the library's answer for a text box that is off is grey the words and leave the box. A shared-layer split was proposed and refuted by that measurement — it would have taken the pressable selector apart to dim a panel the system, asked properly, does not dim.

The TEXT missing was the defect, and it is the 2026-08-22 Card finding word for word: the shared remap rewrites the TONE roles, and this input reads the tone-LESS foreground roles (§11), which no tone rewrite can reach. Measured — a disabled composer's words computed identical to a live one's, so text you had typed stayed at full contrast in a box that had stopped taking keystrokes, which is `readOnly`'s own 2026-08-05 shape. It is also the price of a decision made two paragraphs up: the input is deliberately not a `.kui-control`, so it opted out of the family carrying the remap and lost this with it. `composer.css` states it, keyed on `:disabled` because the input IS the native element (TextArea's third-spelling argument), with the placeholder standing down beside it. Three laws, three sabotage passes; the value law's fixture is a mounted TextArea rather than a colour literal, so a composer inventing its own dead grey fails as loudly as one that dims nothing.

**The ring does not, and the composer states it itself.** Every interactive-surface rule sits on one selector, `:where(button, a, label:has(.kui-control))`, and that selector means *pressable*: it carries the pointer cursor, the 1px rise toward the pointer and the press sink along with the ring. A composer must have none of those. You type in a composer, you do not press it.

That selector is doing two jobs — *this surface is pressable* and *this surface can hold focus* — and a composer is the first thing to want the second without the first. **It does not get split yet.** The house rule is that the second member self-keys and the third promotes (LOG 2026-08-05, TextArea). So `composer.css` writes its own ring, and the split happens when a second focusable, unpressable surface appears.

**The ring keys on the text area, not on the composer.** `:focus-within` narrowed to the input, so pressing the send button inside the composer does not light the whole box. This is TextField's own 2026-08-05 repair, where a hosted control lit two rings and the field claimed a focus it did not have. It is `:focus-within` and not `:focus-visible` because a composer's focus is a mode: the caret is in it whether you clicked or tabbed, which is the field family's sentence.

### Anatomy: four exports

`Composer`, `ComposerInput`, `ComposerRow`, `ComposerSend`. Everything else is a component that already exists.

**`Composer` is a `<form>`.** Both reference implementations are, and v1 is a `div` with `onSubmit` stripped out of its props. The element is what gives Enter-to-submit, `requestSubmit()` and reset, and it is why both references send on Enter by default while v1 needs a `submitOnEnter` prop that defaults to off.

**`ComposerInput` is a bare `<textarea>`, not our TextArea.** TextArea brings its own fill, its own edge and its own ring, and the composer is already all three — you would see two boxes. It exists as a part rather than as a raw element for one non-visual reason: the ring keys on it, so the system has to know which element it is.

It grows with `field-sizing: content`, which reached Baseline on 2026-06-16. Safari is the gap, so `rows` plus a `max-height` is the fallback and both paths get a law. **This is the repair for v1's worst structural fault**: v1 measures with `getComputedStyle`, `scrollHeight`, `requestAnimationFrame` and a `ResizeObserver` on every keystroke, against a rule that says no JS at interaction time. The mechanism did not exist when v1 was written.

**The index reaches the whole unit, and a stated size still wins (REVERSED 2026-08-23, Kushagra: "everything scales, explicit sizes still win").** It shipped pricing the pane and its own text and stopping at the row, on Dialog's ownership argument (§24 against §25) — and read as a LADDER on the preview page that answer did not hold: across four indexes the padding moved four times, the text moved ONCE (it rode `OWNED_BODY_STEP`, which is 14/14/16/16 — sizes 1 and 2 identical, 3 and 4 identical), and no control moved at all. Three of the four steps did nothing but pad the box. **A composer is a unit you size as one thing**, so it supplies `ControlSizeContext` (§28) and its own text takes the plain ladder every other text input uses — 12/14/16/18, four steps for four sizes. `OWNED_BODY_STEP` stays where it belongs: it prices a message the SYSTEM writes to you, and its compression is deliberate for one sentence of explanation; a composer holds a message YOU write. **The widening is Button's, not the composer's** — `control-size.ts` excluded Button by name, arguing that "a button beside that control is a sibling in the form's layout", which is right about a button OUTSIDE the field and is exactly the button React context cannot reach. Inside a unit the rule inverts. A hosted button in a TextField slot is unaffected either way: `--kui-ct-hosted-height` is a registered length declared on the slot, so CSS sizes it from its container whatever index it carries. Four laws, three sabotage passes — the row follows at both extremes, a pinned `size="2"` control keeps it at both, the text ladder has four distinct steps (the sabotage restoring the old map fails printing `14/14/16/16`), and the context does not escape the composer's own subtree.

**`ComposerSend` is one button with four meanings**, driven by a `status` prop: `ready` sends, `submitted` shows a spinner, `streaming` stops, `error` retries. Each state carries its own accessible name.

**It rests LOUD (2026-08-23, Kushagra: *"why is send button not loud?"*).** It shipped taking Button's `medium`, which was an omission rather than a decision — a send button ranking no higher than the model picker beside it names no primary action at all. §11 allows one focal point per surface, and the usual reason a component may not claim it is that it cannot know what else is on the surface; `AlertDialogAction` is the standing exception (§25) and its argument transfers verbatim, because the rule is held by ANATOMY: a composer has exactly one send, the component places it, and every other control in the row belongs to the caller — so the one loud thing is the one the system owns. It is a DEFAULT and not an identity, unlike the alert's pinned pair: the row is the caller's, so the one control the system puts in it stays theirs to re-rank.

This replaces `sendMode` entirely. v1's prop decides whether the button is *visible*; it never decides what the button *means*, so **a person cannot stop a running generation** — the largest gap in the v1 component and one the audit missed. It also fixes v1's split contract, where the Enter key refuses to send an empty message and the button, invisible but still in the tab order, sends one.

**`ComposerRow` SHIPPED, and what it carries is the rhythm rather than the grouping (amended 2026-08-26 — this paragraph listed the part among the section's refusals while it was a public export, which is the drift a law now catches: `src/index.test.ts` walks every name this document says is refused against the barrel, so restating an old refusal as a quotation is not available here — say what the paragraph used to do, do not re-say it).** The first cut refused it because a row of buttons is a `Flex`, and that argument is right about the GROUPING: which controls sit left and which sit right is what those controls mean, which is the app's to say, so v1's `Row`/`RowStart`/`RowEnd` collapse to one part rather than three. What it is wrong about is the alignment, the split and the gap — those are the system's rhythm, and a caller writing `align="center" justify="between" gap="2"` at every call site is the nine-opinions-per-screen §15 exists to stop. So the part states the three distances and nothing else; a caller groups inside it with `Flex`, or drops one flat list in and lets it space evenly.

**The attachment tile is refused *here* and shipped separately.** A file about to be sent and a file already sent are the same tile, so it cannot belong to the composer. It ships as `Attachment`, and it takes its state as a prop — `idle | uploading | processing | error | done` — set by the caller and drawn in CSS. **The system draws the state; the app owns the file.** This is the answer to the audit's open question 2, and it is what v1 gets wrong: v1 holds `File` objects and mints object URLs, then revokes them one commit after handing them to `onSubmit`, so the preview of the message you just sent is already broken.

**Compact is refused for now, and the refusal is the additive direction.** assistant-ui ships a compact composer and the axis is real, so this is a deferral rather than a judgement. But compact only means something when there is something to collapse — their condition is "no attachments, no quote, no queue, no dictation, no newline" — and this component ships none of those. A composer whose control row is always present has nothing to compact into. It ships when something wants it, and when it does it follows their mechanism and not v1's: driven by **content**, never by focus, latching until the text is empty, with no blur handler, emitting one `data-compact` attribute and unmounting nothing. v1 drives the same axis from focus and branches the React tree, which is what produces the bar that can get stuck shut, both slot vocabularies, and the focus that lands on `<body>` when a slot unmounts.

### Glass

**It takes `backdrop`, wired exactly as Card's is.** A composer sitting over a scrolling conversation is the case selective material exists for: something passes behind it (§10, 2026-08-17). It becomes the glass pane and scopes its subtree, so the send button and the attachment tiles inside it resolve solid without any call site saying so. One glass per stack, structurally.

The lens applies. A composer does not fly, so it is built on mount and resize like any other pane.

### What must be falsified before it is trusted

Five, and each is a shape this repo has already been bitten by.

1. **The ring's target.** A law that focuses a Button inside the composer and reads the composer's outline. The fixture must contain a real hosted button, or the law cannot fail — the Notice lesson, twice in one component.
2. **Both growth paths.** The computed height with `field-sizing` and with the `rows` fallback, at the same content. A law that only runs the supported path tests the case that works.
3. **The glass scope.** A law whose fixture holds a button that *would* be glass without the scope. A button with no `backdrop` and no region resolves solid either way, and that exact fixture passed with the scope deleted when Notice shipped.
4. **Enter and IME.** Both references guard `isComposing`; a law drives a composition event, because sending a message mid-composition is silent data loss for anyone typing Japanese, Chinese or Korean.
5. **The clip against the grown box.** `.kui-surface` clips since 2026-08-20. The text area scrolls itself at max height, inside a clipped pane. A law reads the scroll position, because this is the shape that made a select slide its own contents.

**SHIPPED 2026-08-23 — repetition's eighteenth entry, +121 bytes gzipped for the whole component.** (It said "fourteenth" until 2026-08-29, which was Chip's; five components shipped on 2026-08-23 and the ledger was written from two places at once. The order is Chip, Row, Popover, Tooltip, Composer.) Four exports, a column, a ring and a text box, which is what the design predicted: everything a composer LOOKS like is the surface layer's, so `composer.css` names no colour, no corner and no padding of its own.

**15 mounted laws, nine sabotage passes, and one of them SURVIVED.** Deleting `overflow-y: auto` from the text changed nothing — 15 of 15 still green — because the HTML rendering spec already gives a `<textarea>` `overflow: auto`. So the declaration restated a default (one fact, two homes) and the law pinning it could not fail. Both are gone: the declaration, and the assertion. What makes the overflow reachable is the CEILING, the law reads that, and breaking the ceiling fails two laws. Measured rather than assumed — a bare textarea computes `overflow-y: auto` and `resize: both`, which is also what proved `resize: none` load-bearing where its neighbour was not, and it got a sabotage of its own.

**A docs law caught what neither file could see.** The specimen frame wraps an example in a Card unless the example brings its own pane, and it decides by two independent implementations that must agree — one reads the source, the other renders the module. A composer IS a `.kui-surface`, so the rendered half saw a pane and the source half did not, and the frame would have put a pane inside a pane. Neither file contains the nesting; only the rendered tree does, which is exactly the argument that law was written on.

**The index was wrong on the day it shipped, in two directions at once, and Kushagra found it by asking why a size-3 composer left its buttons at 2.** Measured: at size 1 against size 3, the pane's padding moved (12 → 24), a `TextField` dropped in the row moved (1 → 3), a `Button` beside it did not, and **the composer's own text did not** (16px at both, because `composer.css` had hardcoded step 3). Four elements, three behaviours, and the third was chosen by nobody: the `ControlSizeContext` provider reached the row's field-shaped controls as a side effect of being the mechanism Field uses. **The repair that followed — delete the provider, stop the index at the row — was itself reversed later the same day; the paragraph above carries the standing answer.** Both spellings shipped with green suites, because no law read anything at more than two indexes, and 1 against 4 differs under every ladder that has been tried here.

**The repair that ran first, and did not stand (kept because the reasoning is what the standing answer had to beat).** The ownership rule already existed twice — Dialog prices the box alone because it does not own its content (§24); AlertDialog prices everything because it does (§25) — and read that way a composer owns its pane and its text and nothing in its row, so the provider was DELETED and the text was re-pointed at `OWNED_BODY_STEP`. That lasted hours. Read as a LADDER it left half the index doing nothing (14/14/16/16) and left a size-3 composer holding size-2 controls, which is the reversal the earlier paragraph in this section records — **that paragraph carries the standing answer, and this one is history.** As shipped today the composer DOES supply `ControlSizeContext` and its own text takes the plain input ladder, 12/14/16/18.

Two spellings survived the reversal, and both are the shared layer's rather than this file's. The input wears `kui-type` and stamps the step, so the ramp comes from `system/type.css` and the map keeps one home in TypeScript instead of gaining a copy in CSS. And the box is bounded in `lh` — one line and eight — so it is the same number of LINES at every index, where the pinned length it replaced made a size-4 composer shallower in lines than a size-1 one.

**Why the original defect had no law: none of the fifteen read anything at two indexes.** Three do now, and the sabotage pass re-commits each fault — the fixed step, the pinned ceiling, and the row opting back in — and the right law fails each time. −22 bytes gzipped, because the fix DELETES three declarations.

**The gap inside the pane IS the pane's own padding, corrected 2026-08-23 on sight** (Kushagra: the text area and the row "need to have some gap, it doesnt"). It shipped at `--layout-space-3`, which is exactly half the inset at every index, so the text sat 16px from the wall and 8px from the row — measured, the gap was never missing, it was half the distance the eye was already reading as the unit of air, which is what makes a small gap look like none. Judged in the playground against 8, 12 and 16.

The value is `--kui-sf-p` rather than a step, and that is the decision rather than the number: the air inside a composer and the air at its edge are the SAME air, so a second ladder would only be a thing to keep in step with the first. The surface size join already publishes that hook per index on this very element, so the gap answers the index by construction and `composer.css` states no distance of its own. The law asserts the EQUALITY and not a length — a hardcoded 16px agrees at size 2 and nowhere else, and the sabotage pass catches exactly that alongside the original half-step.

### What it does not do

The conversation, the scroller, message parts, branches, reasoning, tool calls, sources, transport and persistence. A model picker is a `Select`. Suggestions are `Button`s. The scroller is a separate decision recorded in `docs/handovers/2026-08-23-chatbar-family.md`: the hard part of a conversation is scroll, and shadcn's is 6,274 lines against 139 of styling.

## 31. Popover (2026-08-23)

**The floating family's fourth member, and the first whose CONTENT the system does not own.** A menu holds rows, a select holds options, an alert holds a fixed question with two answers — each of those is an arrangement this system designed, which is what licenses it to size the type inside them. A popover holds a form, a summary, a colour picker, a filter panel: shapes only the call site knows. So what the component owns is the pane and its behaviour, and everything inside it is the caller's.

**Six flat exports** in shadcn/ui's popover vocabulary (MIT, credited): `Popover`, `PopoverTrigger`, `PopoverContent`, `PopoverTitle`, `PopoverDescription`, `PopoverClose` — the same six Dialog has, for the same reasons. The behaviour is Base UI's Popover; the §20 machinery (Portal → PortalScope → Positioner → Popup, the material read INSIDE the scope) arrives as the family's, with the agreement law every portalling component owes.

### A popover is a CARD that floats, and that split is the section's real content

`kui-floating` was carrying two different claims: **where a pane sits** (it covers the app, so it casts, it expresses the material, it flies out of its trigger) and **what a pane holds** (rows, so its corner is concentric — the rows' corner plus the panel's padding). Every member so far hugged rows, so nothing separated them.

**Measured, the conflation is not subtle.** Wearing the concentric arm, a popover's corner came out **42 / 54.25 / 71.75 / 89.25px** against a Card's 38.7 / 51.6 / 64.5 / 77.4 — rounder than a card at every index, because the sum adds a row corner to card-scale padding with no rows to justify the term. It did not match a Menu either (28 / 33.25 / 36.75 / 40.25), so the "one family, one corner" argument for leaving it alone fails on the same measurement. This is the fraction wall's shape again: a corner holding a fraction of a box the component does not have (§6's checkbox sentence, and Progress's).

**So the concentric arm is keyed on `kui-floating-rows`, not on `kui-floating`** — and the criterion was already written down one join below, in the overlay pane's own comment: *"nothing about it hugs rows, so there is no concentric arithmetic to do."* Menu and Select wear the marker; a floating pane without it falls through to the base surface join and wears the card's corner and the card's padding, which is what it is. **The same defect was there twice**: the squircle bump (`--kui-corner-k: 1.75`, 2026-08-17) is keyed the same way now, because its own sentence says *"a small pane hugging tight rows"* and it was written on `.kui-floating` — with the concentric arm re-keyed and the bump left behind, the corner still came out 42 / 56 / 70 / 84, the same raw band multiplied by 1.75 instead of 1.613.

**What the popover keeps from the floating half is its COVERAGE**: the cast in the ELEVATED world (unlike Dialog it has no scrim to state coverage for it), the material by construction, and the family's entry. In a FLAT world it casts nothing, like every other pane — §22's overlap clause retired 2026-08-19, and a flat popover draws its boundary with the hairline flat regained the day before.

### The page stays live, and that is the whole line between this and Dialog

Nothing is trapped, nothing is dimmed, an outside press dismisses. `modal` is refused for §24's own reason, read backwards: a panel that must be answered before anything else can happen is a `Dialog`, one that stops you to ask a single question is an `AlertDialog`, and those are three different promises that the choice of component is how you make.

**The law for it is the SCRIM, and it took three attempts to find a measurement that was true.** Scroll lock is the obvious one and is not observable — Base UI only locks a document that actually scrolls, so a dialog and a popover both leave `body.style.overflow` untouched in the harness. Inertness was the second, and neither component marks an outside subtree `inert` or `aria-hidden` there. What is always true and visible in the DOM is that a dialog renders a backdrop and a popover renders none. Notice's own shape: the absence is the design.

### What it refuses

**The seed is a CIRCLE on the trigger's centre, faint (2026-08-31, Kushagra: the entry should *"feel like alert dialog's"*).** §22's silhouette is the right first frame where the panel IS the thing pressed, lifting — a menu is its button's list. A popover's content is the caller's and shares no shape with its trigger, so the trigger's pill swelling into a form read as the button inflating. It takes the alert's seed, paint and CLOCKS — the family's designed circle at `50%` so it stays curvy as it opens, arriving at opacity 0, on the overlay's fall, spread, becoming, reveal and reveal delay re-pointed token for token on the popup — and keeps the floating family's origin: the circle sits on the trigger's centre, which is what still says where the panel came from. Self-keyed in popover.css over the family's pose; the runner is untouched, and two registered hooks (`--kui-seed-dx/dy`) tell the family's seed translate how far inside the measured trigger box the circle sits. The seed does not touch `corner-shape`: the glint's mask reads it on the premise that it never changes in flight. Rejected: the alert's recipe verbatim (rises from below its own footprint and forgets the anchor), a fade on the silhouette alone, and the seed without the clocks (judged: the box was landed before the circle could be seen). Tooltip takes neither grammar: its entry is a LIFT at its own size (§32). LOG carries the three shapes as put to him.

**An arrow.** Base UI ships one and it stays unrendered. An arrow is a second boundary on a pane whose boundary is already stated — the 2026-08-07 doubled-edge defect, and on glass it would need its own veil, ring and lens for a shape the lens cannot describe (the map encodes one rounded rect). What says where the panel came from is that it is anchored to the trigger and flies out of its silhouette; the system's answer to that question is motion, not a beak.

**A width floored at the trigger.** Menu floors at `--anchor-width` because "never narrower than the button you pressed" is a size-match argument about a list of that button's own options. A popover holds a form, so its width is its content's; tying it to whatever opened it would make one panel a different shape per call site.

**Free positioning** (a side and an alignment, and the system does the rest — §23's sentence), and **a drawn ✕** (Dialog's refusal, verbatim: two other ways out, and a corner glyph on a small pane competes with what the panel is for).

### What it takes from the index

`size` prices the BOX — padding and corner — and not the type inside it, which is Dialog's rule and holds here for the same reason. The exception is `PopoverTitle` and `PopoverDescription`: those exist only because `aria-labelledby` and `aria-describedby` need something to point at (§10's anatomy criterion, its one sanctioned shape), so they are type the SYSTEM owns and they read the shared `OWNED_*_STEP` map — a popover, a dialog and an alert at one index are one typography.

### The CONTENT scrolls, the panel never does (recorded 2026-08-29)

Adopted the day after the component shipped — Menu's own 2026-08-17 move, one member over — and this section did not say so until the ultracode audit found the omission. `PopoverContent` wraps the caller's children in a system `ScrollArea`, so the pane keeps its glass, its corner and its cast while the viewport inside it carries the overflow. The pane also caps itself at `--available-height` / `--available-width`, with the cap restated on the popup's own viewport.

**Why the component and not the call site.** Before it, the sheet's own comment RECOMMENDED a ScrollArea to the caller — a component asking a call site to solve a problem only the component can see, because `--available-height` is a fact the positioner reports to the pane and a caller wrapping its own content cannot read it. An overlong panel clipped its own bottom with no way to reach it.

**`focusable` is left alone, and that is the one place it departs from Menu.** Menu passes `focusable={false}`: ARIA voids `role="presentation"` on a focusable element, and a roving-focus widget must not grow a tab stop inside itself — which it can afford because the menu scrolls its own highlight into view. A popover holds arbitrary content and traps nothing, so nothing else will scroll it, and a keyboard user reaching a scrollable region needs it focusable (WCAG 2.1.1). Two different answers because they are two different panels.

**The consequence a maintainer needs**: the ScrollArea is what makes surfaces.css's `:has(> .kui-scroll-area:only-child)` column rule fire on this pane, and it is why a caller-composed scroller inside a popover is a special case (its scroll position is deliberately not reset by the entry's revocation, which reaches only the direct-child chain).

**16 mounted laws, four sabotage passes; +14 bytes gzipped** for the component and the corner split together. **31 laws since 2026-08-29**, when the audit found the material path — `useMaterial({ backdrop: true })`, `useLensRef` and `GlassScope`, all three — with no reader anywhere in the suite: three sabotages left twenty-three laws green.


---

## 32. Tooltip (2026-08-23)

**The name of a control, shown to a pointer that rests on it — and the last of the five floating components §20 named on the day the portal groundwork shipped** (Menu, Select, Popover, Dialog, Tooltip; AlertDialog joined by the split).

### It may only restate what the control already announces

This is the rule the whole component hangs on rather than a nicety, and every other decision below follows from it. A tooltip has no keyboard route, no touch route and no reading order: it is an affordance of exactly one input device. So anything appearing only here is information lost to everybody else, and the component is safe precisely to the extent that its words are the accessible name the trigger already carries. An icon-only button with `aria-label="Undo"` and a tooltip reading "Undo" is the shape this exists for — and it is the shape apps/docs was already in, with 26 icon-only buttons whose only name was an attribute.

**That is also why it has no touch story and does not need one.** There is no hover on a phone, so nothing opens; because the tooltip never carried anything of its own, nothing is missing. This is what §17 and §18 both recorded as the open *hover-reveal → tap* question, and the answer is that a well-formed tooltip has nothing to adapt. A hint a touch user genuinely needs is a `FieldDescription`, a `Notice`, or words on the screen.

**It is HIDDEN from assistive technology, which is that rule enforced rather than written down.** Base UI wires nothing — measured, the trigger carries no `aria-describedby` and the panel no `role="tooltip"` — so the choice was ours either way. Announcing a restatement produces "Undo, button, Undo"; and if a tooltip carries something its control does not, hiding it creates no new problem, because that information was already unavailable to every keyboard and touch user. The repair is to put the words somewhere they live.

### The inversion, and it mints nothing

§11 has said `neutral (inverted)` with `exception: high-contrast inverted` since the defaults table was written — the only row in that table that ever named an exception, and it had never been built. It costs two declarations and no new colour: **the pane paints itself in `--color-text` and its words are written in `--color-surface`**, so it is dark on a light page and light on a dark one, at the highest contrast the palette has, in whatever appearance is in effect. `contrast="high"` reaches it for free, and the component knows nothing about it — though ONE of the two roles is what delivers that, not both (corrected 2026-08-29, ultracode audit). `--color-text` chains to `--neutral-12`, which every high-contrast scope re-declares; `--color-surface` is declared once per appearance and no HC scope touches it or anything it reads. The pair therefore SEPARATES rather than both moving: light drives the fill #1f1f21 → #030304 against an ink already at the palette's white end, dark drives it #ebebec → #fafafa. Contrast rises in both modes, so the guarantee stands and no HC arm is owed here; only the explanation was wrong, in three homes at once.

**A `<Theme appearance>` flip was the first idea and it cannot work**: under `appearance="inherit"` — the supported shape, and what apps/docs itself renders — no React code knows which mode is in force, because the answer is on `<html>` and arrives before hydration. The role pair needs no such knowledge.

**The inversion is declared on two elements, and it has to be.** `--color-text` cannot be re-scoped on the pane, because the fill READS it and a property cannot invert the value its own declaration consumed. The floating family's body element already sits between the pane and the content, so the role flips there.

**And that is exactly as far as an inverted pane can reach**, which is why `children` is typed as a `string`. A component that stamps a tone re-declares the ink roles ON ITS OWN ELEMENT and outranks anything an ancestor re-scoped — measured, a `Kbd` inside a tooltip kept the page's dark ink and its own pale fill and vanished on a near-black pane. More re-scoping does not fix it (the fill would still be wrong), and inverting the whole palette needs the appearance nobody knows. So a tooltip holds a sentence; the shortcut case is a string too (`Undo ⌘Z`), and anything that genuinely needs a chip in it is a `Popover`.

### The box, and the two absences

`--tooltip-p-block` and `--tooltip-p-inline` are a PAIR of picks into layout space, block tighter than inline — the row family's own finding that a one-line box asks a different question on each axis. Picks rather than raw lengths, so density reaches a tooltip through the layer with nothing designed twice. `--tooltip-max-w` caps it: a tooltip that runs the width of the window is a paragraph. There is no `min-width` floor, because a tooltip is as wide as its words.

**No size axis** (Progress's decision, one family over): the only index a tooltip could ride is its TRIGGER's, which it cannot see and which would make one label two sizes. It does stamp a fixed `data-size="1"` — without an index the surface join never fires and the pane falls back to a card's un-indexed corner, measured at 64.52px on a 30px chip.

**No material, ever.** The material defends a foreground against what passes behind a pane; a tooltip defends itself by inverting, which is the stronger answer, and two defences on one 28px box is the doubled-edge defect's shape. It is also not a `kui-floating-rows` pane (§31): it hugs one line of text, so there is no concentric arithmetic to do.

### The delay belongs to a region

`TooltipProvider` states it, because that is the only place Base UI accepts it (measured: the Root has no `delay` in 1.7) — and that turns out to be the right home anyway. A delay is a property of a REGION of the interface, not of one label, and the provider does a second thing worth the export: it GROUPS, so the first tooltip in a toolbar waits and the rest arrive as the pointer travels. It is optional; a lone tooltip works on Base UI's own timing. It is excluded from the builder's catalog with that reason written down, because configuration for a region is not a thing you place in a composition.

### The entry is a lift, not a silhouette (2026-08-31)

**Kushagra: the family's unfurl is "way too much, especially when it's on a larger surface, it looks very weird for a tooltip" — and the "Physics" tooltip of the Clip-vs-Physics bench is the reference.** Measured on a 420px card trigger before the change, the chip's first frame was the card's full box collapsing ~370px of width on the spread clock into a 60px chip; on a button it swelled a button into a strip and back. §22's silhouette is honest where the panel IS the thing pressed; a tooltip is a label, and a label is not the thing it names.

**The bench's answer, built out of the family's own channels with nothing new in the runner.** The chip arrives at its own size and shape, already at its resting place, scaled to `--tooltip-seed` (0.9) about the trigger-facing edge — the origin the family's table already answers for every (side, align, dir) cell — and faint; it grows to full size on the family's spring while it paints. Four things are stated in `tooltip.css`, each a re-aim of an existing channel: the pose reads the LANDED box (`--kui-fly-w/h/r`) instead of the trigger's photograph; `translate: 0 0` (the box is already home); the body's molten print is stood down whole (no blur, no fade, no echo, no squish — the chip and its words are one object, since the shape is known from frame one); and the chip states its OWN transition list — three channels, not the family's seven: scale on `--tooltip-form` (300) and the CALM spring, opacity on `--tooltip-paint` (120), the cast on the family's reveal — so the runner's release, which waits for the longest declared clock, lands with the chip instead of holding the pose 200ms past it. **The curve is the second pass** (Kushagra on the first: *"Doesnt seem the same physics tho"*): frozen beside the bench's tooltip at the same times, opacity was identical and scale was not — the family's channel rides the elastic spring (ζ0.715, ω10.8), at 0.995 by 90ms and landed at 120 with no tail, where the bench's `--spring` is this package's `--motion-spring` (calm, ζ0.65, ω7.7; the samples are byte-identical) and is still at 0.983 at 90, crossing 1 near 130 and easing back through 1.004. Same clock, a faster spring — ours popped where the bench settles. An easing is a token NAME inside the family's list and cannot be re-pointed without renaming a system curve on the element, which is why the tooltip owns its list; after the change the two match within 0.001 at every frame. And the exit returns to the SEED (0.9) rather than the family's 2% settle — the bench's distance, on the family's settle clock and its stiff spring rather than the bench's `ease-in` (an exit that ends at maximum velocity slams; at 120ms over a tenth of scale the distance is what the eye reads). A scale rather than a length because scale is relative: 3px on a 28px chip and the same proportion on the widest label the cap allows. Warm groups are untouched: `data-instant="delay"` still zeroes every clock, which is the bench's warm half. Stood down under reduced motion at its own weight — the family's guard names `.kui-surface.kui-floating` and this list outranks it.

**Two things the mechanism forced.** The family's centred pin (`inset-inline-start: 50%` + `transform: translateX(-50%)`) composes INSIDE the individual `scale`, so on a scaled box the half-width pull is scaled with it — measured, the chip's centre 4.4px off at the seed and sliding home; a full-size box in a positioner sized to it needs no pin, so every inset is `0` and the transform `none` for the flight. And the runner's laid-out guard reads the posed rect against the natural one — a pose the same size as its box would read as "not laid out yet" and bail — which the scaled pose passes because a rect includes its transform; the law asserts the flight departs. The tooltip's rules carry `[aria-hidden]` plus the placement stamps for RANK over the family's `[dir="rtl"]` centred arms, `aria-hidden` being the tooltip's own permanent stamp.

**Not built: the bench's warm SLIDE** — one tooltip travelling and resizing between adjacent triggers. Base UI mounts one popup per tooltip, so a slide is a measured cross-element flight and a runner mechanism of its own; a warm tooltip appears instantly today, which is what every platform does. Recorded open.

**Five mounted laws, six sabotage passes each caught by exactly its law** (the pose deleted, the list's spring swapped for the elastic, the exit rule deleted, the body stand-down deleted, the pin stand-down deleted, the clocks borrowed back); the curve law compares the running transition's `linear()` SAMPLES with the token's, because the browser re-serializes the string; the seed is read at station 0 of the SEIZED scale clock rather than raced for, and the fixture is a trigger far wider than the chip, because on a button-sized trigger the silhouette and the landed chip are nearly one box (the degenerate-fixture rule, and the exact case the complaint names). Two floating-family laws that had been written on a tooltip fixture moved to a popover, since the tooltip's box no longer passes through any width or height. +68 bytes gzipped.

**15 mounted laws at shipping, five compile-time refusals, six sabotage passes; +117 bytes gzipped.** The delay, the inset pair and the width cap are v0 for the eye pass.


## 33. Tree (spec'd 2026-08-26; the TREE vocabulary SHIPPED the same day — the gate's record is at the end)

**One pattern, one machine: hierarchical content revealed and hidden by the person using it.** The spec exists because two sentences in this repo pointed opposite directions — §27 records *"a shell sidebar with a tree built in is a kookie block, not kookie ui"* (2026-08-20) while the docs-site entry calls the nav's disclosure block a stand-in for *"the tree the package will ship"* (2026-08-21, Kushagra himself: *"kookie will ship a tree too, not today"*). Both are right, about different layers, and this section is the reconciliation: **the MACHINE is the package's, the INSTRUMENT is a block** — the code block's own three-way split (LOG 2026-08-26) one pattern over. A layers panel with rename and drag, a file browser with icons: blocks, built on this. The disclosure state, the keyboard, the announcement, the indent: the package's, because the ARIA tree pattern is a spec rather than a taste, and a spec copied into app source is exactly what rots (Field's argument, §28).

**The depth-1 case is not a separate component.** A "collapsible nav group" — a sidebar section that opens and closes — was almost spec'd as its own small part, and THESIS §2 is what killed the split (Kushagra: to a user it IS a tree; it has subcontents): pattern first, encapsulation second, name last, and carving by the names "NavGroup" and "Tree" is the industry's inversion. One machine; a two-level docs nav is the shallow rendering of it. Base UI's `Collapsible` is not this — it is one disclosure with no traversal, no levels and no selection, and building the nav on it commits to a second implementation the day the nav grows a third level.

### What the pattern forces (in scope)

- **Disclosure per node**, controlled and uncontrolled.
- **The keyboard machine, verbatim from the ARIA tree pattern**: Up/Down over VISIBLE nodes, Right expands or descends, Left collapses or ascends, Home/End, typeahead. Not designed — transcribed; the design freedom here is zero and that is the argument for owning it once.
- **Two announcement vocabularies, per member** (the row family's shape: shared paint, per-member facts): an app instrument announces `role="tree"`/`treeitem` with `aria-level`/`aria-setsize`/`aria-posinset`; a NAVIGATION tree announces as a nav landmark with `aria-expanded` sections and `aria-current` links, because the ARIA APG itself separates disclosure navigation from tree views and `role="tree"` on a nav is over-claiming.
- **Selection, single AND multi.** Machine-owned `aria-selected` with `aria-multiselectable` and Shift-arrow range extension — spec'd, not invented. The paint is the shared `[data-checked], [data-selected]` vocabulary (§23), the same names the chosen Card reads, so a selected tree row and a chosen card light through one rule. Multi was OUT of the first draft of this spec and the chosen-card work is what reversed it (2026-08-26, Kushagra): the system already spells selection through real primitives and shared vocabulary, and the named first consumer — the builder's Layers pane — already holds a multi-selection list with a primary member. A spec whose first consumer needs the feature cannot defer it.
